\varphi(\partial),\varphi(x)
are of subrectangular type, then we call such
\varphi
of subrectangular type. An operator
P\in A_{1}
whose Newton Polygon
N
is of subrectangular type we’ll call of subrectangular type too.

For an operator
P\in A_{1}
of subrectangular type as above, we’ll call the monomial
Hm(P):=x^{l}y^{k}
as
the highest monomial
of
P
.

It’s easy to see that for any positive pair
(\sigma,\rho)
,
f_{\sigma,\rho}(\varphi(\partial,x))
are monomials, if
\varphi
is of subrectangular type.

Remark 3.1
.

Note that for two operators
P_{1},P_{2}
of subrectangular type the operator
P_{1}P_{2}
is also of subrectangular type. Indeed, by theorem
2.2
we have
f_{1,0}(P_{1}P_{2})=f_{1,0}(P_{1})f_{1,0}(P_{2})
and

f_{0,1}(P_{1}P_{2})=f_{0,1}(P_{1})f_{0,1}(P_{2})
, so the Newton polygon contains a monomial
Hm(P_{1})Hm(P_{2})
, and clearly this monomial is the highest monomial with respect to the weights
v_{1,0}
and
v_{0,1}
, i.e.
v_{1,0}(P_{1}P_{2})=v_{1,0}(Hm(P_{1})Hm(P_{2}))
and
v_{0,1}(P_{1}P_{2})=v_{0,1}(Hm(P_{1})Hm(P_{2}))
. So,

P_{1}P_{2}
is of subrectangular type with the highest monomial
Hm(P_{1})Hm(P_{2})
.

Recall one definition from
[
25
]
:

Definition 3.2
.

Suppose
\varphi
is an endomorphism of subrectangular type. Let
(\sigma,\rho)
be any two positive integer numbers.

Assume
f_{\sigma,\rho}(\varphi(\partial))=c_{1}x^{l}y^{k}
,
f_{\sigma,\rho}(\varphi(x))=c_{2}x^{l^{\prime}}y^{k^{\prime}}
, where
l,k,l^{\prime},k^{\prime}
are non-zero integers,
c_{1},c_{2}\in{\mbox{\dbl C}}
. By
[
13
, L. 2.7]
,
f_{\sigma,\rho}(\varphi(\partial))
is proportional to
f_{\sigma,\rho}(\varphi(x))
. Define
\epsilon(\varphi)\in\mathbf{Q}
as

(l^{\prime},k^{\prime})=\epsilon(\varphi)(l,k)

We’ll call this
\epsilon(\varphi)
as the rate of
\varphi(\partial)
with
\varphi(x)
. Note that
\epsilon(\varphi)
does not depend on
(\sigma,\rho)
.

A pair of operators
P,Q\in A_{1}
with the property
[Q,P]=1
giving a
counterexample
to the Dixmier conjecture will be called as a
DC-pair
(in spirit of
[
21
]
). That is,
P=\varphi(x)
,
Q=\varphi(\partial)
for some
\varphi\in\mathop{\rm End}(A_{1})\backslash\mathop{\rm Aut}(A_{1})
.

Assume
(P,Q)
is a DC-pair. Then according to
[
21
, Prop. 6.9]
we can assume without loss of generality that
P,Q
and the corresponding endomorphism
\varphi
are of subrectangular type and that
P,Q
is a minimal such pair. Then
Hm(P)=x^{a}y^{p}
, for some
a,p\in{\mbox{\dbl N}}
. By
[
21
, Th. 4.1 (3)]

a\neq p
. Without loss of generality (applying the Fourier transform if necessary) we can assume
a<p
. Besides, applying if necessary a generic linear change of variables
x\mapsto x+\varepsilon
,
\partial\mapsto\partial
we can assume without loss of generality that
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(P)=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(P)=p
and
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q)=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(Q)=q
. In particular,
HT(P)\in{\mbox{\dbl C}}[x]
is a polynomial with non-zero free term:
HT(P)(0)\neq 0
.

Put
p=dn
,
q=dm
, where
(n,m)=1
12
12

12

Here and further we assume that
(n,m)=1
means
GCD(m,n)=1
and
m,n>1
.
,
d=GCD(p,q)
. By
[
13
, L. 2.7]
the highest terms of
P
and
Q
are proportional:
f_{0,1}(P)^{m}=\tilde{c}f_{0,1}(Q)^{n}
for some
\tilde{c}\in{\mbox{\dbl C}}^{*}
. In particular,
n|\deg_{x}(f_{0,1}(P))
. Assume
\deg_{x}(f_{0,1}(P))=nl
. Denote by
h\in{\mbox{\dbl C}}[x]
the coefficient at
y^{p}
in
f_{0,1}(P)
:
f_{0,1}(P)=hy^{p}
. Note that
h=g^{n}
for some
g\in{\mbox{\dbl C}}[x]
with
\deg_{x}g=l
.
Since
nl=a<p=nd
, we must have
d>1
and
l<d
. Analogously,
Hm(Q)=x^{ml}y^{md}
and the rate
\epsilon(\varphi)=n/m
. Since
P,Q
is minimal,
\epsilon\notin{\mbox{\dbl N}}
.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
\varphi
be an endomorphism of subrectangular type,
P=\varphi(x)
,
Q=\varphi(\partial)
, with highest monomials
Hm(P)=x^{ln}y^{dn}
,
Hm(Q)=x^{lm}y^{dm}
, where
d>1
,
l<d
,
(n,m)=1
(so,
d=GCD(\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(P),\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q))
,
l=GCD(\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{x}(P),\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{x}(Q))
).

Define
essential GCD of
P
,
Q
as

EssGCD(P,Q):=\frac{d}{GCD(l,d)}>1.

Recall one lemma from
[
25
]
:

Lemma 3.1
.

(
[
25
, Lem. 3.2]
)

Suppose
\varphi
is an endomorphism of subrectangular type,
\epsilon=\epsilon(\varphi)
.

Assume
P\in A_{1}
,
P\notin{\mbox{\dbl C}}
is such that
f_{\epsilon,1}(P)
is a monomial. Then for any
\sigma,\rho>0
the polynomial
f_{\sigma,\rho}(\varphi(P))
is a monomial with

v_{\sigma,\rho}(\varphi(P))=(\rho k+\sigma l)v_{\epsilon,1}(P),

(3.1)

where
f_{\sigma,\rho}(\varphi(\partial))=c_{1}x^{l}y^{k}
,
f_{\sigma,\rho}(\varphi(x))=c_{2}x^{l\epsilon}y^{k\epsilon}
.

Corollary 3.1
.

Under assumptions of lemma let
f_{\epsilon,1}(P)=cx^{u}y^{v}
,
c\in{\mbox{\dbl C}}^{*}
. Then the Newton polygon of
\varphi(P)
is of subrectangular type and
Hm(\varphi(P))=x^{l(u\epsilon+v)}y^{k(u\epsilon+v)}
.

Proof.

Just note that for any monomial
x^{u^{\prime}}\partial^{v^{\prime}}
we have
\varphi(x^{u^{\prime}}\partial^{v^{\prime}})=\varphi(x)^{u^{\prime}}\varphi(\partial)^{v^{\prime}}
and by
[
13
, L. 2.7]
we have therefore

f_{\sigma,\rho}(\varphi(x^{u^{\prime}}\partial^{v^{\prime}}))=\tilde{c}x^{l(u^{\prime}\epsilon+v^{\prime})}y^{k(u^{\prime}\epsilon+v^{\prime})}

for any positive
\sigma,\rho
and some
\tilde{c}\in{\mbox{\dbl C}}^{*}
.
For all non-zero monomials
c^{\prime}x^{u^{\prime}}\partial^{v^{\prime}}
from
P
we have, by assumption,

v_{\epsilon,1}(c^{\prime}x^{u^{\prime}}\partial^{v^{\prime}})=u^{\prime}\epsilon+v^{\prime}\leq v_{\epsilon,1}(cx^{u}\partial^{v})=u\epsilon+v,

and the equality holds only for the monomial
cx^{u}\partial^{v}
,
i.e.
v_{\epsilon,1}(P)=u\epsilon+v
and moreover, the monomial
x^{l(u\epsilon+v)}y^{k(u\epsilon+v)}
belongs to the Newton polygon of
\varphi(P)
.

From (
3.1
) we have
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{x}(\varphi(P))\leq lv_{\epsilon,1}(P)
and
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(\varphi(P))\leq kv_{\epsilon,1}(P)
, i.e. the Newton Polygon of
\varphi(P)
is contained in the rectangle constructed by
\{(0,0),(0,k(u\epsilon+v)),(l(u\epsilon+v),0),(l(u\epsilon+v),k(u\epsilon+v))\}
. Since the vertex
(l(u\epsilon+v),k(u\epsilon+v))
belongs to the Newton Polygon of
\varphi(P)
, we are done.
∎

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\varphi
be an endomorphism of subrectangular type,
\epsilon=\epsilon(\varphi)
,
P=\varphi(x)
,
Q=\varphi(\partial)
, with highest monomials
Hm(P)=x^{ln}y^{dn}
,
Hm(Q)=x^{lm}y^{dm}
, where
d>1
,
l<d
,
(n,m)=1
.

Let
\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{I}\}
,
\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{J}\}
be the set of non-zero coefficients at the monomials of
P,Q
respectively. Let
F(X,Y)=\sum_{i,j}\alpha_{ij}X^{i}Y^{j}\in{\mbox{\dbl Q}}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{I},q_{1},\ldots,q_{J})[X,Y]
be a non-zero polynomial with coefficients in the field
K:={\mbox{\dbl Q}}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{I},q_{1},\ldots,q_{J})\subset{\mbox{\dbl C}}
.

Let
\Phi_{N,\lambda}
be a tame automorphism of
A_{1}
such that
\lambda\in{\mbox{\dbl C}}
is not algebraic over the field
K
and
N>\epsilon
.

Then the operator
\varphi\circ\Phi_{N,\lambda}(F(P,Q))\in A_{1}
is of subrectangular type
13
13

13

Here
F(P,Q):=\sum_{i,j}\alpha_{ij}X^{i}Y^{j}|_{X\mapsto P,Y\mapsto Q}
.
and

\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(\varphi\circ\Phi_{N,\lambda}(F(P,Q)))=dmv_{\epsilon,1}(\Phi_{N,\lambda}(F(P,Q)))=dm\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(\Phi_{N,\lambda}(F(P,Q))).

Proof.

First note that for any monomial
x^{l}\partial^{k}
the operator
\Phi_{N,\lambda}(x^{l}\partial^{k})
has the
(N,1)
-top line trough the point
(l,k)
, and this line contains a non-zero monomial on the vertical
y
-axis, whose coefficient is a polynomial in
\lambda
(maybe of zero degree) with integer coefficients.

Let
l_{N,1}(F(P,Q))
be the
(N,1)
-top line of the operator
F(P,Q)
. Since all coefficients of monomials belonging to
l_{N,1}(F(P,Q))
are from
K
, the
(N,1)
-top line
l_{N,1}(\Phi_{N,\lambda}(F(P,Q)))
of the operator
\Phi_{N,\lambda}(F(P,Q))
contains a non-zero monomial on the vertical
y
-axis, say
c_{1}y^{M}
(because all monomial on
l_{N,1}(F(P,Q))
have different degrees in the variable
x
and
\lambda
is not algebraic over
K
).

Then we have
v_{N,1}(c_{1}\partial^{M})=M=v_{\epsilon,1}(c_{1}\partial^{M})
, but since
N>\epsilon
, for all other monomials of the line
l_{N,1}(\Phi_{N,\lambda}(F(P,Q)))
we have

M=v_{N,1}(x^{l}\partial^{k})=Nl+k>\epsilon l+k=v_{\epsilon,1}(x^{l}\partial^{k}),

and for all other monomials of
\Phi_{N,\lambda}(F(P,Q))
away from the line
l_{N,1}(\Phi_{N,\lambda}(F(P,Q)))
we have

M>v_{N,1}(x^{l^{\prime}}\partial^{k^{\prime}})=Nl^{\prime}+k^{\prime}\geq\epsilon l^{\prime}+k^{\prime}=v_{\epsilon,1}(x^{l^{\prime}}\partial^{k^{\prime}}).

So, the conditions of lemma
3.1
are satisfied (
f_{\epsilon,1}(\Phi_{N,\lambda}(F(P,Q)))=c_{1}y^{M}
is a monomial) and therefore by corollary
3.1
the operator
\varphi\circ\Phi_{N,\lambda}(F(P,Q))
is of subrectangular type and

\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(\varphi\circ\Phi_{N,\lambda}(F(P,Q)))=dmv_{\epsilon,1}(\Phi_{N,\lambda}(F(P,Q)))=dm\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(\Phi_{N,\lambda}(F(P,Q))).

∎

Remark 3.2
.

Note that the assertion of lemma
3.2
is true also for
F(X,Y)\in{\mbox{\dbl C}}[X,Y]
and any
\lambda\neq 0
if it is known that
F(P,Q)
is of subrectangular type. Indeed, in this case the
(N,1)
-top line of
F(P,Q)
has a unique vertex, and therefore the
(N,1)
-top line of the operator
\Phi_{N,\lambda}(F(P,Q))
contains a non-zero monomial on the vertical
y
-axis. The rest of the proof is the same.

In fact, we’ll use lemma
3.2
exactly for this situation. However, we decided to formulate this lemma in a more general form for the convenience of further references in future works.

Corollary 3.2
.

In the assumptions of remark
3.2
(i.e.
\varphi
satisfies assumptions of lemma
3.2
,
N>\epsilon
and
\lambda\in{\mbox{\dbl C}}^{*}
) put
\hat{P}:=\varphi\circ\Phi_{N,\lambda}(P)
,
\hat{Q}:=\varphi\circ\Phi_{N,\lambda}(Q)
. Then

\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(\hat{P})=dm(nd+Nln),\quad\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(\hat{Q})=dm(md+Nlm)

and the operators
\hat{P},\hat{Q}
are of subrectangular type with
EssGCD(\hat{P},\hat{Q})=EssGCD(P,Q)
.

Proof.

By remark
3.2
and lemma we know that
\hat{P},\hat{Q}
are of subrectangular type with

Hm(\hat{P})=x^{lm(nd+Nln)}y^{dm(nd+Nln)},\quad Hm(\hat{Q})=x^{lm(md+Nlm)}y^{dm(md+Nlm)}.

Hence

GCD(\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(\hat{P}),\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(\hat{Q}))=dm(d+Nl),\quad GCD(\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{x}(\hat{P}),\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{x}(\hat{Q}))=lm(d+Nl)

and therefore

EssGCD(\hat{P},\hat{Q})=\frac{dm(d+Nl)}{m(d+Nl)GCD(l,d)}=\frac{d}{GCD(l,d)}=EssGCD(P,Q).

∎

4
The main preparatory theorems

The aim of this section is to prove the following theorem and a corollary – theorem
4.2
. In this section we will work with the first Weyl algebra
A_{1}
over the field
ℂ
(it may be assumed to be any other algebraically closed field of characteristic zero).

Theorem 4.1
.

Assume
(P,Q)
is a subrectangular DC-pair (i.e.
P=\varphi(x)
,
Q=\varphi(\partial)
for some endomorphism
\varphi
of
A_{1}
of subrectangular type) with
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(P)=p
and
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q)=q
. Assume
p=dn
,
q=dm
,
d>1
,
(n,m)=1
,

Hm(P)=x^{ln}y^{dn}
,
l<d
, and
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(P)=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(P)
,
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q)=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(Q)
. Then
Hm(Q)=x^{ml}y^{md}
and the rate
\epsilon(\varphi)=n/m
(see section
3
).

Let
\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{I}\}
,
\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{J}\}
be the set of non-zero coefficients at the monomials of
P,Q
respectively. Put
K:={\mbox{\dbl Q}}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{I},q_{1},\ldots,q_{J})\subset{\mbox{\dbl C}}
.

Then there exist sequences of natural numbers
n_{0},\ldots,n_{I}
,
m_{0},\ldots,m_{I}
, a sequence of constants
\varepsilon_{0},\ldots,\varepsilon_{I}\in K
14
14

14

This statement about the field of definition of constants is not essential for the proof of theorem
1.1
, however, we decided to formulate this theorem in a more general form for the convenience of further references in future works.
The numbers
n_{i},m_{i},\epsilon_{i}
are not uniquely defined.
, and a sequence of polynomials
F_{i}(X,Y)\in K[X,Y]
,
i=0,\ldots,I
defined by recursion as
F_{i+1}(X,Y)=F_{i}(X,Y)^{n_{i}}-\varepsilon_{i}^{n_{i}}X^{m_{i}}
, where
F_{0}(X,Y):=Y
15
15

15

These polynomials determine also a sequence of operators in
A_{1}
after substitution
X\mapsto P
,
Y\mapsto Q
. We’ll denote this sequence also as
F_{i}(P,Q)
.
, such that

d_{2}:=EssGCD(P,Q)\quad|\quad GCD\{\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(P),\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(F_{0}(P,Q)),\ldots,\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(F_{i}(P,Q))\}\quad|\quad d,\quad\mbox{for $i<I$},

\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(F_{I}(P,Q))=1\mbox{\hskip 10.22217ptmod\hskip 10.22217pt}GCD\{\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(P),\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(F_{0}(P,Q)),\ldots,\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(F_{I-1}(P,Q))\}.

Moreover, we have the equality

\sum_{i=0}^{I-1}(n_{i}\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits F_{i}(P,Q)-\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits F_{i+1}(P,Q))=p+q-1,

(4.1)

and for all
i=0,\ldots,I
we have
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits F_{i}(P,Q)=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}F_{i}(P,Q)
.

Remark 4.1
.

In fact, the proof of this theorem will provide also an alternative proof of the following result, which is already known:
16
16

16

I learned from Leonid Makar-Limanov that it can be proved with the help of the same technique that was used in particular in papers
[
29
]
,
[
22
]
to show that the Newton polygon
N(f)
of a JC-pair
(f,g)
(giving a counterexample to the JC conjecture for
K[x,y]
) can be assumed to be included in a trapezium with
one vertex
v
, two edges parallel to the
y
-axis and to the bisectrix of the first quadrant adjacent to
v
, and two edges belonging to the coordinate axes, cf.
[
34
]
and references therein. I’d like to thank Christian Valqui who kindly informed me how this result can be deduced from their work
[
22
]
.
let
f_{0,1}(P)=g^{n}y^{p}
, where
g\in K[x]
is a polynomial with
g(0)\neq 0
.

Then
g
has only one root, i.e.
g=(ax+b)^{l}
for some
a,b\in K
17
17

17

Besides, the proof of our theorem works also in the case
l=d
, thus we get an alternative proof of
[
21
, Th. 4.1 (3)]
.
. Moreover, from the proof we’ll see that for any
i=0,\ldots,I

f_{0,1}(F_{i}(P,Q))=(ax+b)^{l_{i}}y^{d_{i}}
for some
l_{i},d_{i}\in{\mbox{\dbl N}}
.

Besides, the theorem holds also in the case
l>d
, if
d
does not divide
l
. The last assumption is necessary for the condition
d_{2}>1
; it is also essential in lemma
4.3
.

Proof.

Because of our assumptions on
P,Q
(namely, that
g(0)\neq 0
) there exists an automorphism
\Phi_{u}
of the ring
D_{1}=K[[x]][\partial]
of the form

\Phi_{u}(x)=u\in K[[x]],\quad\Phi_{u}(\partial)=\frac{1}{u^{\prime}}\partial+v,

(4.2)

where
u(0)\neq 0
,
u^{\prime}(0)\neq 0
and
v\in K[[x]]
, such that
P^{\prime}:=\Phi_{u}(P)\in D_{1}
18
18

18

Starting from this point onwards we use a different (from section
2
) notation for a normal form of
Q
with respect to
P
: instead of
Q^{\prime}
we’ll use
\tilde{Q}
.
is normalized, i.e.
P^{\prime}=\partial^{p}+b_{p-2}\partial^{p-2}+\ldots+b_{0}
, see e.g.
[
7
, Prop. 1.3]
. Then, obviously,
Q^{\prime}:=\Phi_{u}(Q)=c\partial^{q}+l.o.t.
for some
c\in K
. Since
[\varepsilon Q^{\prime},\varepsilon^{-1}P^{\prime}]=1
for any
\varepsilon\in{\mbox{\dbl C}}^{*}
, composing, if necessary,
\Phi_{u}
with an appropriate scale transform
\partial\mapsto\varepsilon\partial
,
x\mapsto\varepsilon^{-1}x
, such that
c\varepsilon^{q+p}=1
, taking the DC-pair
\varepsilon^{-p}P,\varepsilon^{p}Q
instead of
(P,Q)
(and extending
K
by
c^{-1/(p+q)}
), we can assume without loss of generality that
P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}
are both monic differential operators. Observe that

\Phi^{-1}_{u}(\partial)=g^{1/d}\partial+v^{\prime}

(4.3)

for an appropriate
v^{\prime}\in K[[x]]
, and
\Phi^{-1}_{u}(x)=f\in K[[x]]
, where
f^{\prime}:=\partial(f)=g^{-1/d}
, as
[\Phi^{-1}(\partial),f]=1
, and
f(0)=0
. Note that
\Phi_{u}
preserves the order filtration on
D_{1}
, i.e.
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(\Phi_{u}(Y))=\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Y)
for any
Y\in D_{1}
.

Idea of proof:
we use the theory of normal forms from section
2
and calculate first a normal form (any normal form) of the operator
Q^{\prime}
with respect to
P^{\prime}
. This normal form has a very simple structure – it is a sum of an operator from the centraliser
C(\partial^{p})\subset\hat{D}_{1}^{sym}
of
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}
-order
q
and a homogeneous operator of
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}
-order
-p
, which is uniquely determined and whose shape can be explicitly calculated (let’s call it the ”tail”). Constructing by induction the operators
F_{i}(P,Q)
and using specific properties of the Schur operator for
P^{\prime}
(the condition
A_{p}(0)
and conditions on the first two homogeneous components
S_{0},S_{-1}
) as well as the Dixmier lemma
[
13
, L. 2.7]
(theorem
2.2
), we show that the orders of operators
F_{i}(P,Q)
satisfy the property claimed in the theorem. For the recursive construction of operators it is important that original operators,
P,Q
, are of subrectangular type: first, this is necessary for application of the Dixmier lemma, second, this condition is used to show that the inductive process stops. The crucial step here is a specific differential equation on polynomials (see lemma
4.3
).

The proof is divided into 3 steps: in step 1 we calculate the ”tail” of the normal form of
Q^{\prime}
with respect to
P^{\prime}
, in step 2 we construct
F_{1}
and prove its properties needed for the induction procedure, and in step 3 we continue the inductive procedure and construct other operators
F_{i}
.

More precisely, to construct
F_{i}
, we first consider a normal form
\tilde{Q}
of
Q^{\prime}
with respect to
P^{\prime}
(i.e. we choose some Schur operator
S
for
P^{\prime}
) and then construct in steps 2 and 3, by the recursive procedure of ”head chopping”, the operators
\tilde{U}_{i}
, which are the normal forms of the operators
F_{i}(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
with respect to
P^{\prime}
. Using the specific properties of the Schur operator for
P^{\prime}
(which hold because
P^{\prime}
is normalized), we can check, by induction, the corresponding properties of orders of the operators
\tilde{U}_{i}
. Again due to the specific properties of the Schur operator, by conjugating back the operators
\tilde{U}_{i}
and applying the automorphism
\Phi_{u}^{-1}
, we obtain the needed properties of orders of the operators
F_{i}(P,Q)
(as such a conjugation and the automorphism preserve orders) at the end of step 3.

Step 1.
By proposition
2.2
there exists a Schur operator
S\in\hat{D}_{1}^{sym}
with
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(S)=0
such that
P^{\prime}=S^{-1}\partial^{p}S
and
S_{0}=1
,
S_{-1}=0
. Then by corollary
2.1
we have also
S^{-1}_{0}=1
,
S_{-1}^{-1}=0
. Put
\tilde{P}:=SP^{\prime}S^{-1}=\partial^{p}
,
\tilde{Q}:=SQ^{\prime}S^{-1}
. Then by proposition
2.1
, item 2 the homogeneous decomposition of
\tilde{Q}
can be written as

\tilde{Q}=\partial^{q}+\tilde{Q}_{q-1}+\ldots+\tilde{Q}_{-p},

where
[\tilde{Q}_{j},\partial^{p}]=0
for
-p+1\leq j\leq q-1
and therefore
\tilde{Q}_{j}=(\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}c_{ij}A_{p;i})D^{j}
, where
c_{ij}\in\tilde{K}=K[\xi]
, and
[\tilde{Q}_{-p},\partial^{p}]=1
.

Lemma 4.1
.

In the notation above we have

\tilde{Q}_{-p}=(-\frac{1}{p}x\partial+\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}e_{i}A_{p;i})\int^{p},

where
e_{k}=\frac{1}{p(\xi^{-k}-1)}
for
k=1,\ldots,p-1
,
e_{0}=\frac{p-1}{2p}
. Here
\xi
is a primitive
p
-th root of unity.

The vector form
\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q}_{-p})
has the shape

\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q}_{-p})=(-\frac{1}{p}\Gamma_{1}+(0,\frac{1}{p},\frac{2}{p},\ldots,\frac{p-1}{p}))\tilde{D}^{-p}.

Proof.

First note that by proposition
2.1
the operator
\tilde{Q}_{-p}
is uniquely defined by the property
[\tilde{Q}_{-p},\partial^{p}]=1
if it exists. Let’s show that the operator from formulation satisfies this property. We have by lemma
2.2

[\tilde{Q}_{-p},\partial^{p}]=1+\frac{1}{p}x\partial(B_{1}+\ldots+B_{p})-(\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}e_{i}A_{p;i})(B_{1}+\ldots+B_{p})=\\
1+\frac{1}{p}(B_{2}+2B_{3}+\ldots+(p-1)B_{p})-(\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}e_{i})B_{1}-(\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}e_{i}\xi^{i})B_{2}-\ldots-(\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}e_{i}\xi^{(p-1)i})B_{p}=\\
1-(\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}e_{i})B_{1}-(\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}e_{i}\xi^{i}-\frac{1}{p})B_{2}-\ldots-(\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}e_{i}\xi^{(p-1)i}-\frac{p-1}{p})B_{p}

Hence we need to show that
(e_{0},\ldots,e_{p-1})
is a solution of the linear system

\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}1&1&\ldots&1\\
1&\xi&\ldots&\xi^{p-1}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
1&\xi^{p-1}&\ldots&\xi^{(p-1)^{2}}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}e_{0}\\
e_{1}\\
\vdots\\
e_{p-1}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
\frac{1}{p}\\
\vdots\\
\frac{p-1}{p}\end{array}\right)

with the Vandermonde matrix
W
. It is well known that the inverse of the Vandermonde matrix
W^{-1}=(b_{ij})
, where
b_{ij}=\frac{1}{p}\xi^{(1-i)(j-1)}
,
i,j=1,\ldots,p
. Therefore for
k=1,\ldots,p
we have

e_{k-1}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}b_{kj}\frac{j-1}{p}=\frac{1}{p^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\xi^{(1-k)(j-1)}(j-1)=\frac{1}{p^{2}}(1+x+\ldots+x^{p})^{\prime}|_{x=\xi^{1-k}}=\frac{1}{p(\xi^{-k}-1)},

and
e_{0}=\frac{p-1}{2p}
as stated.

The last claim is straightforward.
∎

Step 2.
(The first induction step) Recall the notation:
\hat{R}=K[[x]]
.

Lemma 4.2
.

In the notation above put
\tilde{U}_{1}:=\tilde{Q}^{n}-\tilde{P}^{m}
. Then we have

1.

U_{1}^{\prime}:=S^{-1}\tilde{U}_{1}S\in D_{1}
and
\Phi_{u}^{-1}(U_{1}^{\prime})\in A_{1}
;

2.

the operator
[\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{1}]
satisfies condition
A_{p}(0)
;

3.

\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})>q(n-1)-p
;

4.

\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})>q(n-1)-p+1
if the polynomial
g
has more than one root.

Moreover, if
g
has only one root and
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})=q(n-1)-p+1
, then
\sigma(\tilde{U}_{1})=\varepsilon_{1}\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})}
with

\varepsilon_{1}=\frac{\alpha^{-1}}{d}(1-\frac{l}{d}),

where
g=(1+\alpha x)^{l}
, and

U_{1}^{\prime}=a_{q(n-1)-p+1}\partial^{q(n-1)-p+1}+a_{q(n-1)-p}\partial^{q(n-1)-p}+\ldots+a_{0}

with
a_{q(n-1)-p+1}=\varepsilon_{1}+\frac{1}{d}x
and
a_{i}\in\hat{R}
for
i<q(n-1)-p+1
.

5.

If
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})>q(n-1)-p+1
, then the operator
\tilde{U}_{1}
satisfies condition
A_{p}(0)
;

6.

if
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})>q(n-1)-p+1
, then the number
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})
is divisible by
d_{2}:=\frac{d}{GCD(l,d)}>1
; in particular,
d_{2}
divides
GCD(\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{P}),\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{Q}),\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1}))
(and this GCD divides
d
).

7.

Sdeg_{A}((\tilde{U}_{1})_{j})\leq 0
for
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})\geq j>q(n-1)-p
and
Sdeg_{A}((\tilde{U}_{1})_{q(n-1)-p})=1
. Besides,

(\tilde{U}_{1})_{q(n-1)-p}=c_{1}x\partial^{q(n-1)-p+1}+c_{0}\partial^{q(n-1)-p},

where
c_{1}\in K
and
c_{0}
is a HCP from
Hcpc(p)
with
Sdeg_{A}(c_{0})\leq 0
and
Sdeg_{B}(c_{0})=-\infty
.

8.

M_{1}:=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})-(q(n-1)-p)<q+p=:M_{0}
.

Proof.

Item
1. is obvious (
U_{1}^{\prime}=(Q^{\prime})^{n}-(P^{\prime})^{m}
).

Item
2. We have

[\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{1}]=[\tilde{P},\tilde{Q}]\tilde{Q}^{n-1}+\tilde{Q}[\tilde{P},\tilde{Q}]\tilde{Q}^{n-2}+\ldots+\tilde{Q}^{n-1}[\tilde{P},\tilde{Q}]=-n\tilde{Q}^{n-1}.

Therefore,
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}([\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{1}])=q(n-1)
. Since
\tilde{Q}_{q}=\partial^{q}
, we have
(\tilde{Q}^{n-1})_{q(n-1)}=\partial^{q(n-1)}
and therefore
Sdeg_{A}([\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{1}]_{q(n-1)})=0
. By lemma
2.7
all homogeneous components of
[\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{1}]
are totally free of
B_{j}
, because
\tilde{Q}
satisfies condition
A_{p}(0)
by proposition
2.5
. By lemma
2.8

\tilde{Q}^{n-1}
satisfies condition
A_{p}(0)
, i.e. the operator
[\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{1}]
satisfies condition
A_{p}(0)
.

Items
3., 4. From item 2. we immediately get
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})\geq q(n-1)-p
. Assume that
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})\leq q(n-1)-p+1
, i.e.
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})=q(n-1)-p+1
or
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})=q(n-1)-p
.

1). By item 2. we know that
Sdeg_{A}([\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{1}]_{q(n-1)-t})<t
for all
t>0
. Therefore,

Sdeg_{A}(\tilde{U}_{1})_{q(n-1)-p-t}<t+1

for all
t>0
.

2). Note that
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(U_{1}^{\prime})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})
(by remark
2.1
). In the first case (i.e.
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})=q(n-1)-p+1
) let’s prove that

(U_{1}^{\prime})_{q(n-1)-p+1}=(\tilde{U}_{1})_{q(n-1)-p+1}=\varepsilon_{1}\partial^{q(n-1)-p+1},

where
0\neq\varepsilon_{1}\in K
. Indeed, for all
j>q(n-1)-p
we have

(\tilde{Q}^{n})_{j}=\sum_{i_{1}+\ldots+i_{n}=j}\tilde{Q}_{i_{1}}\cdots\tilde{Q}_{i_{n}},

(4.4)

where
i_{k}\leq q
. Since
j>q(n-1)-p
, we have
i_{k}>-p
for all
k
. Therefore,
Sdeg_{A}(\tilde{Q}_{i_{k}})\leq 0
for all
k
and by lemma
2.6
we get
Sdeg_{A}((\tilde{Q}^{n})_{j})\leq 0
for all
j>q(n-1)-p
.
Therefore
Sdeg_{A}((\tilde{U}_{1})_{j})\leq 0
for all
j>q(n-1)-p
too. Since
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})=q(n-1)-p+1
, we must have
Sdeg_{A}((\tilde{U}_{1})_{q(n-1)-p+1})=0
.

Now note that

(\tilde{U}_{1})_{q(n-1)-p+1}=(\tilde{Q}^{n})_{q(n-1)-p+1}=(U_{1}^{\prime})_{q(n-1)-p+1}\in D_{1}
because
S^{-1}_{0}=S_{0}=1
, i.e. it is a differential operator, and by lemmas
2.1
,
2.3
this HCP does not depend on
A_{p;i}
, i.e. it looks like
\varepsilon_{1}\partial^{q(n-1)-p+1}
for
\varepsilon_{1}\in K
.

3). By lemma
4.1
we have

(\tilde{U}_{1})_{q(n-1)-p}=(\tilde{Q}^{n})_{q(n-1)-p}=\tilde{Q}_{-p}\partial^{q(n-1)}+\partial^{q}\tilde{Q}_{-p}\partial^{q(n-2)}+\ldots+\partial^{q(n-1)}\tilde{Q}_{-p}+\\
\sum_{i_{1}+\ldots+i_{n}=q(n-1)-p\atop i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}>-p}\tilde{Q}_{i_{1}}\cdots\tilde{Q}_{i_{n}}=\frac{1}{d}x\partial^{q(n-1)+1-p}+\\
(n\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}e_{nj}A_{p;nj})\partial^{q(n-1)-p}+\sum_{i_{1}+\ldots+i_{n}=q(n-1)-p\atop i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}>-p}\tilde{Q}_{i_{1}}\cdots\tilde{Q}_{i_{n}},

(4.5)

where the last equality holds because the HCP
(\tilde{Q}^{n})_{q(n-1)-p}
is totally free of
B_{j}
. Note that all terms in the last raw of this formula have
Sdeg_{A}\leq 0
by lemma
2.6
.

4). Put
\tilde{\tilde{U}}_{1}:=\tilde{U}_{1}-\frac{1}{d}x\partial^{q(n-1)+1-p}
. In the first case (i.e.
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})=q(n-1)-p+1
) this operator satisfies condition
A_{p}(0)
. Indeed, conditions 1,2 from definition
2.9
are obvious, the symbol
\sigma(\tilde{\tilde{U}}_{1})
does not contain
A_{p;i}
and
Sdeg_{A}(\sigma(\tilde{\tilde{U}}_{1}))=0
by 2), and
Sdeg_{A}((\tilde{\tilde{U}}_{1})_{q(n-1)-p+1-t})<t
for all
t>0
by 3), 1).

Then

U_{1}^{\prime}=S^{-1}\tilde{\tilde{U}}_{1}S+\frac{1}{d}S^{-1}x\partial^{q(n-1)+1-p}S.

By lemma
2.8
the operator
S^{-1}\tilde{\tilde{U}}_{1}S
satisfies condition
A_{p}(0)
and

S^{-1}x\partial^{q(n-1)+1-p}S
satisfies condition
A_{p}(1)
. Therefore, by lemma
2.4

Sdeg_{A}(U_{1}^{\prime})_{q(n-1)-p-t}<t+1

for all
t>0
, and therefore

\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(U_{1}^{\prime})_{q(n-1)-p-t}=q(n-1)-p-t+Sdeg_{A}(U_{1}^{\prime})_{q(n-1)-p-t}<q(n-1)-p+1

for all
t>0
. On the other hand, since
(S^{-1})_{-1}=S_{-1}=0
and
(S^{-1})_{0}=S_{0}=1
, we have

(U_{1}^{\prime})_{q(n-1)-p}=\frac{1}{d}x\partial^{q(n-1)+1-p}+(\tilde{\tilde{U}}_{1})_{q(n-1)-p},

and therefore

U_{1}^{\prime}=a_{q(n-1)-p+1}\partial^{q(n-1)-p+1}+a_{q(n-1)-p}\partial^{q(n-1)-p}+\ldots+a_{0}

with
a_{q(n-1)-p+1}=\varepsilon_{1}+\frac{1}{d}x
and
a_{i}\in\hat{R}
for
i<q(n-1)-p+1
.

5). In the second case (i.e.
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})=q(n-1)-p
) by steps 1), 3) we get for all
t\geq 0

Sdeg_{A}(\tilde{\tilde{U}}_{1})_{q(n-1)-p-t}<t+1.

Consider
U_{1}^{*}:=\tilde{\tilde{U}}_{1}+\partial^{q(n-1)-p+1}
. Then
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(U_{1}^{*})=q(n-1)-p+1
, and
U_{1}^{*}
satisfies condition
A_{p}(0)
. We have
\tilde{U}_{1}=U_{1}^{*}-\partial^{q(n-1)-p+1}+\frac{1}{d}x\partial^{q(n-1)-p+1}
, hence

U_{1}^{\prime}=S^{-1}U_{1}^{*}S+\frac{1}{d}S^{-1}x\partial^{q(n-1)-p+1}S-S^{-1}\partial^{q(n-1)-p+1}S.

As in step 4), by lemma
2.8
the operators
S^{-1}U_{1}^{*}S
,
S^{-1}\partial^{q(n-1)-p+1}S
satisfy condition
A_{p}(0)
, and the operator
S^{-1}x\partial^{q(n-1)-p+1}S
satisfies condition
A_{p}(1)
.
Therefore, by lemma
2.4

Sdeg_{A}(U_{1}^{\prime})_{q(n-1)-p-t}<t+1
for any
t>0
, and therefore for any
t>0

\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(U_{1}^{\prime})_{q(n-1)-p-t}=q(n-1)-p-t+Sdeg_{A}(U_{1}^{\prime})_{q(n-1)-p-t}<q(n-1)-p+1.

On the other hand, since
(S^{-1})_{-1}=S_{-1}=0
and
(S^{-1})_{0}=S_{0}=1
, we have

(U_{1}^{\prime})_{q(n-1)-p}=\frac{1}{d}x\partial^{q(n-1)-p+1}+(\tilde{\tilde{U}}_{1})_{q(n-1)-p}

and therefore
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(U_{1}^{\prime})_{q(n-1)-p}=q(n-1)-p+1
(as
Sdeg_{A}(\tilde{\tilde{U}}_{1})_{q(n-1)-p}\leq 0
) and

U_{1}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{d}x\partial^{q(n-1)-p+1}+a_{q(n-1)-p}\partial^{q(n-1)-p}+\ldots+a_{0}

with
a_{i}\in\hat{R}
for
i<q(n-1)-p+1
.

6). Recall that
\Phi_{u}^{-1}(U_{1}^{\prime})\in A_{1}
(as
\Phi_{u}^{-1}(U_{1}^{\prime})=Q^{n}-P^{m}
). For convenience denote
z:=q(n-1)-p
. Then in the second case (i.e.
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})=q(n-1)-p
) the highest coefficient of the differential operator
\Phi_{u}^{-1}(U_{1}^{\prime})
will be equal to
H:=\frac{1}{d}fg^{(z+1)/d}\in x\cdot K[x]
, and in the first case (i.e.
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})=q(n-1)-p+1
) it will be equal to
\tilde{H}:=(\varepsilon_{1}+\frac{1}{d}f)g^{(z+1)/d}\in K[x]
. So, either
\frac{1}{d}f=Hg^{-(z+1)/d}
or

\varepsilon_{1}+\frac{1}{d}f=\tilde{H}g^{-(z+1)/d}
. In both cases, differentiating these equalities with respect to
x
we get (we’ll use the same notation
H
both for
H
and
\tilde{H}
below)

\frac{1}{d}f^{\prime}=\frac{1}{d}g^{-1/d}=H^{\prime}g^{-(z+1)/d}-\frac{z+1}{d}Hg^{-1-(z+1)/d}g^{\prime},

whence

\frac{1}{d}g^{(m-1)(n-1)}=H^{\prime}g-\frac{z+1}{d}Hg^{\prime}.

(4.6)

Claim:
\deg_{x}(H)=(m-1)(n-1)l-(l-1)
. Proof: since
\deg_{x}g^{(m-1)(n-1)}=l(m-1)(n-1)
, we have

\deg_{x}(H)\geq(m-1)(n-1)l-(l-1)
. Obviously,
\deg_{x}H^{\prime}g=\deg_{x}Hg^{\prime}
, and the highest coefficients of
H^{\prime}g
and
Hg^{\prime}
are
\deg_{x}(H)c_{H}c_{g}
and
lc_{H}c_{g}
correspondingly, but
GCD(z+1,d)=1
and
d\nmid l
, therefore
\deg_{x}(H)c_{H}c_{g}-\frac{z+1}{d}lc_{H}c_{g}\neq 0
whence

\deg_{x}(H^{\prime}g-\frac{z+1}{d}Hg^{\prime})=\deg_{x}Hg^{\prime}
and thus
\deg_{x}(H)=(m-1)(n-1)l-(l-1)
.
19
19

19

Note that if
l=d
, these arguments imply that (
4.6
) is impossible, because
(m-1)(n-1)d-(d-1)=z+1
,
i.e.
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})>q(n-1)-p+1
.

7). Let’s show that the identity (
4.6
) is possible only if
g
has one root. This follows from the auxiliary lemma:

Lemma 4.3
.

Suppose
\tilde{g}\in{\mbox{\dbl C}}[x]
is a polynomial with more than one root. Suppose
\deg_{x}(\tilde{g})=\tilde{l}
, and
\tilde{d},\tilde{z},A\in{\mbox{\dbl N}}
, satisfy the following relation:
\tilde{z}=(A-1)\tilde{d}
.

Then there exists a solution
H\in{\mbox{\dbl C}}[x]
of the differential equation

\tilde{c}\tilde{g}^{A}=H^{\prime}\tilde{g}-\frac{\tilde{z}+1}{\tilde{d}}H\tilde{g}^{\prime},\quad\tilde{c}\in{\mbox{\dbl C}}^{*}

(4.7)

only if
\tilde{l}/\tilde{d}\in{\mbox{\dbl N}}
,
\tilde{l}/\tilde{d}>1
. In this case
\deg_{x}(H)=\tilde{l}(\tilde{z}+1)/\tilde{d}
.

Proof.

If
H
exists, clearly we have

\deg_{x}(H)\geq A\tilde{l}-(\tilde{l}-1)=\tilde{l}(A-1)+1
.

Let, say

\tilde{g}=c(x-\alpha_{1})^{j_{1}}\ldots(x-\alpha_{k})^{j_{k}}(x-\beta_{1})\ldots(x-\beta_{p}),

where
k>1
or
p>1
or
k=p=1
(if
p<1
or
k<1
we assume there are no factors with
\beta_{j}
or
\alpha_{j}
) and all
j_{q}>1
. Then

g_{1}:=GCD(\tilde{g},\tilde{g}^{\prime})=\begin{cases}c(x-\alpha_{1})^{j_{1}-1}\ldots(x-\alpha_{k})^{j_{k}-1}&k\geq 1\\
1&k<1.\end{cases}

Assume
\tilde{g}=g_{1}r
,
\tilde{g}^{\prime}=g_{1}s
, where

r=(x-\alpha_{1})\ldots(x-\alpha_{k})(x-\beta_{1})\ldots(x-\beta_{p}),

s=\sum_{q=1}^{k}j_{q}(x-\alpha_{1})\ldots\widehat{(x-\alpha_{q})}\ldots(x-\alpha_{k})(x-\beta_{1})\ldots(x-\beta_{p})+\\
\sum_{q=1}^{p}(x-\alpha_{1})\ldots(x-\alpha_{k})(x-\beta_{1})\ldots\widehat{(x-\beta_{q})}\ldots(x-\beta_{p}).

By our assumptions
\deg_{x}s>0
, and, obviously,
GCD(r,s)=1
and
\deg_{x}(r)=\deg_{x}(s)+1
. Dividing equation (
4.7
) by
g_{1}
, we get the equation

\tilde{c}g_{1}^{A-1}r^{A}=H^{\prime}r-\frac{\tilde{z}+1}{\tilde{d}}Hs.

Since
GCD(r,s)=1
and the left hand side has the same roots as
r
,
H
is divisible by all linear factors of
r
. Let’s show that
H
must be divisible by the whole left part, i.e.

\deg_{x}(H)\geq\tilde{l}A-\deg_{x}(g_{1})>\tilde{l}(A-1)+1.

Note that this is possible only if
\deg_{x}(H^{\prime}\tilde{g}-\frac{\tilde{z}+1}{\tilde{d}}H\tilde{g}^{\prime})<\deg_{x}H\tilde{g}^{\prime}
, i.e. only if
\deg_{x}(H)=\tilde{l}\frac{\tilde{z}+1}{\tilde{d}}
and
{\mbox{\dbl N}}\ni\tilde{l}/\tilde{d}>1
.

The proof is by induction on the degree: let, say,
H=(x-\alpha_{1})H_{1}
(so that
\deg_{x}H_{1}=\deg_{x}H-1
). Then, dividing the equation above by
(x-\alpha_{1})
we get the new equation on
H_{1}
:

G=H_{1}^{\prime}r-\frac{\tilde{z}+1}{\tilde{d}}Hs_{1},\quad s_{1}=s-\frac{\tilde{d}}{\tilde{z}+1}r/(x-\alpha_{1}),

where the set of roots of
G
belong to the set of roots of
r
again. Note that
s_{1}
has the same shape as
s
:

s_{1}=(j_{1}-\frac{\tilde{d}}{\tilde{z}+1})(x-\alpha_{2})\ldots(x-\alpha_{k})(x-\beta_{1})\ldots(x-\beta_{p})+\\
\sum_{q=2}^{k}j_{q}(x-\alpha_{1})\ldots\widehat{(x-\alpha_{q})}\ldots(x-\alpha_{k})(x-\beta_{1})\ldots(x-\beta_{p})+\\
\sum_{q=1}^{p}(x-\alpha_{1})\ldots(x-\alpha_{k})(x-\beta_{1})\ldots\widehat{(x-\beta_{q})}\ldots(x-\beta_{p}).

Since
\frac{\tilde{d}}{\tilde{z}+1}<1
, we have
GCD(r,s_{1})=1
and therefore we can repeat these arguments for other linear divisors of
G
.

Continuing this line of reasoning, we’ll get generic equations of the following type:

G_{i}=H_{i}^{\prime}r-\frac{\tilde{z}+1}{\tilde{d}}H_{i}s_{i},

where

s_{i}=\sum_{q=1}^{k}(j_{q}-\frac{\tilde{d}}{\tilde{z}+1}n_{q})(x-\alpha_{1})\ldots\widehat{(x-\alpha_{q})}\ldots(x-\alpha_{k})(x-\beta_{1})\ldots(x-\beta_{p})+\\
\sum_{q=1}^{p}(1-\frac{\tilde{d}}{\tilde{z}+1}m_{q})(x-\alpha_{1})\ldots(x-\alpha_{k})(x-\beta_{1})\ldots\widehat{(x-\beta_{q})}\ldots(x-\beta_{p}),

with
n_{q}\leq j_{q}A-j_{q}
(note that the multiplicity of
\alpha_{q}
in
g_{1}^{A-1}r^{A}
is
(j_{q}-1)(A-1)+A=j_{q}A-j_{q}+1
),
m_{q}\leq A-1
, and

G_{i}=\tilde{c}\frac{g_{1}^{A-1}r^{A}}{(x-\alpha_{1})^{n_{1}}\ldots(x-\alpha_{k})^{n_{k}}(x-\beta_{1})^{m_{1}}\ldots(x-\beta_{p})^{m_{p}}},

so that the set of roots of
G_{i}
always belong to the set of roots of
r
. Then,

j_{q}-\frac{\tilde{d}}{\tilde{z}+1}n_{q}=\frac{j_{q}d(A-1)+j_{q}-dn_{q}}{\tilde{z}+1}\geq\frac{j_{q}}{\tilde{z}+1}>0,

1-\frac{\tilde{d}}{\tilde{z}+1}m_{q}=\frac{\tilde{d}(A-1)+1-\tilde{d}m_{q}}{\tilde{z}+1}\geq\frac{1}{\tilde{z}+1}>0,

whence
GCD(r,s_{i})=1
. At the last step we get the equation

c(x-\alpha_{1})\ldots(x-\alpha_{k})(x-\beta_{1})\ldots(x-\beta_{p})=H_{N}^{\prime}r-\frac{\tilde{z}+1}{\tilde{d}}H_{N}s_{N}

whence again
H_{N}
must be divisible by the polynomial on the left hand side, and we are done.

∎

8). If
g
has only one root, then
g=(1+\alpha x)^{l}
for some
\alpha\in K
. In this case a solution of equation (
4.6
) necessarily has the form
H=\tilde{c}(1+\alpha x)^{l(m-1)(n-1)-l+1}
,
\tilde{c}\in K
. But in the second case (i.e.
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})=q(n-1)-p
)
H(0)=0
- a contradiction, so item 2. holds. In the first case (i.e.
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})=q(n-1)-p+1
) it maybe possible. Besides, in this case

f=\alpha^{-1}(1-\frac{l}{d})(1+\alpha x)^{1-l/d}+c_{0},

where
c_{0}=-\alpha^{-1}(1-\frac{l}{d})
(as
f(0)=0
). Then necessarily

\varepsilon_{1}=-\frac{c_{0}}{d}=\frac{\alpha^{-1}}{d}(1-\frac{l}{d}).

Item
5. By the same arguments as in item 3. 2) we get
(U_{1}^{\prime})_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(U_{1}^{\prime})}=(\tilde{Q}^{n})_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(U_{1}^{\prime})}=(\tilde{U}_{1})_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})}\in D_{1}
and therefore
(U_{1}^{\prime})_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(U_{1}^{\prime})}=\varepsilon_{1}\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})}
with
0\neq\varepsilon_{1}\in K
, because
Sdeg_{A}((\tilde{U}_{1})_{j})\leq 0
for all
j>q(n-1)-p
(see (
4.4
) and explanations in 3. 2)).

By item 2. we know that
Sdeg_{A}([\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{1}]_{q(n-1)-t})<t
for all
t>0
. Therefore,

Sdeg_{A}(\tilde{U}_{1})_{q(n-1)-p-t}<t+1

for all
t>0
. Since
\chi:=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})>q(n-1)-p+1
, we get

Sdeg_{A}(\tilde{U}_{1})_{\chi-t}<t

for all
t>0
. In particular, the operator
\tilde{U}_{1}
satisfies condition
A_{p}(0)
.

Item
6. Since the operator
\tilde{U}_{1}
satisfies condition
A_{p}(0)
, the operator
U_{1}^{\prime}
also satisfies condition
A_{p}(0)
by proposition
2.5
. Therefore,
U_{1}^{\prime}
is a formally elliptic differential operator (cf. similar arguments in 3., 4),5)), i.e.
U_{1}^{\prime}=\varepsilon_{1}\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})}+\ldots
, and therefore,
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(U_{1}^{\prime})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(U_{1}^{\prime})
.

Analogously,

[P^{\prime},U_{1}^{\prime}]=S^{-1}[\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{1}]S\in D_{1}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathop{\rm\bf ord}([P^{\prime},U_{1}^{\prime}])=q(n-1).

By proposition
2.5

[P^{\prime},U_{1}^{\prime}]
satisfies condition
A_{p}(0)
. Since
U_{1}^{\prime},[P^{\prime},U_{1}^{\prime}]
are differential operators, their homogeneous components are also differential operators, i.e. they don’t depend on
A_{p,i}
, cf. lemma
2.3
. Then, for any
t>0
we have

\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits([P^{\prime},U_{1}^{\prime}]_{q(n-1)-t})=Sdeg_{A}([P^{\prime},U_{1}^{\prime}]_{q(n-1)-t})+q(n-1)-t<q(n-1),

and
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits([P^{\prime},U_{1}^{\prime}]_{q(n-1)})=q(n-1)=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}([P^{\prime},U_{1}^{\prime}])
, i.e. the highest term of the
differential operator

[P^{\prime},U_{1}^{\prime}]
coincides with
[P^{\prime},U_{1}^{\prime}]_{q(n-1)}=-n\partial^{q(n-1)}
.

Put
Q_{1}:=\Phi_{u}^{-1}(U_{1}^{\prime})
. Note that
Q_{1}\in A_{1}
and
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{1})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(Q_{1})=\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(U_{1}^{\prime})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(U_{1}^{\prime})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})
, because
\Phi_{u}^{-1}
preserves the degree filtration on
D_{1}
and
g(0)\neq 0
. Now we have

\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits([P,Q_{1}])=\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits\Phi_{u}^{-1}([P^{\prime},U_{1}^{\prime}])=\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits([P^{\prime},U_{1}^{\prime}])=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}([P^{\prime},U_{1}^{\prime}])=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}([\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{1}]).

Since by items 3., 2. we have
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(P)+\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{1})-1>q(n-1)=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}([\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{1}])
, it follows by
[
13
, L. 2.7]
that
f_{0,1}(P)
is proportional with
f_{0,1}(Q_{1})
, i.e.
f_{0,1}(Q_{1})^{p}=\tilde{c}f_{0,1}(P)^{\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{1})}=\tilde{c}f_{0,1}(P)^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})}
. Therefore,
l\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})
is divisible by
d
, i.e.
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1})
is divisible by
d_{2}
.

Items
7., 8. immediately follow from item 3., 4. (cf. formulae (
4.4
), (
4.5
)).

∎

Step 3.
The proof of our theorem will follow by induction from lemma below.

Lemma 4.4
.

In the notation above suppose
\tilde{U}_{k}\in\hat{D}_{1}^{sym}
(
k\geq 1
) is an operator which satisfies the following properties (below we assume
n_{0}:=n
):

1.

U_{k}^{\prime}:=S^{-1}\tilde{U}_{k}S\in D_{1}
and
\Phi_{u}^{-1}(U_{k}^{\prime})\in A_{1}
;

2.

the operator
\tilde{U}_{k}
satisfies condition
A_{p}(0)
, i.e. in particular
(\tilde{U}_{k})_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}\tilde{U}_{k}}=\varepsilon_{k}\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}\tilde{U}_{k}}
for some
\varepsilon_{k}\in K^{*}
;

3.

the operator
[\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{k}]
satisfies condition
A_{p}(0)
;

4.

\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})>\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k-1})n_{k-1}-M_{k-1}+1
;

5.

The number
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})
is divisible by
d_{2}=EssGCD(P,Q)
;

6.

Sdeg_{A}(\tilde{U}_{k})_{j}\leq 0
for
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})\geq j>\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k-1})n_{k-1}-M_{k-1}
,

and
Sdeg_{A}(\tilde{U}_{k})_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k-1})n_{k-1}-M_{k-1}}=1
. Besides,

(\tilde{U}_{k})_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k-1})n_{k-1}-M_{k-1}}=c_{k}x\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k-1})n_{k-1}-M_{k-1}+1}+c_{k-1}\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k-1})n_{k-1}-M_{k-1}},

where
c_{k}\in K
and
c_{k-1}
is a HCP from
Hcpc(p)
with
Sdeg_{A}(c_{k-1})\leq 0
.

7.

M_{k}:=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})-(\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k-1})n_{k-1}-M_{k-1})<M_{k-1}
.

In the notation above put
n_{k}:=\frac{p}{GCD(p,\mathop{\rm\bf ord}\tilde{U}_{k})}
,
m_{k}:=\frac{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}\tilde{U}_{k}}{GCD(p,\mathop{\rm\bf ord}\tilde{U}_{k})}
,

\tilde{U}_{k+1}:=\tilde{U}_{k}^{n_{k}}-\varepsilon_{k}^{n_{k}}\tilde{P}^{m_{k}}.

Then we have

1.

U_{k+1}^{\prime}:=S^{-1}\tilde{U}_{k+1}S\in D_{1}
and
\Phi_{u}^{-1}(U_{k+1}^{\prime})\in A_{1}
;

2.

the operator
[\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{k+1}]
satisfies condition
A_{p}(0)
;

3.

\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})>\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}
;

4.

\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})>\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}+1
if the polynomial
g
has more than one root;

Moreover, if
g
has only one root and
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}+1
, then

\sigma(\tilde{U}_{k+1})=\varepsilon_{k+1}\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})}
, and

U_{k+1}^{\prime}=a_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}+1}\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}+1}+a_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}}\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}}+\ldots+a_{0}

with
a_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}+1}=\varepsilon_{k+1}+c_{k+1}x
and
a_{i}\in\hat{R}
for
i<\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}+1
.

5.

if
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})>\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}+1
, then the operator
\tilde{U}_{k+1}
satisfies condition
A_{p}(0)
;

6.

if
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})>\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}+1
, then the number
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})
is divisible by
d_{2}
; in particular,
d_{2}
divides
GCD(\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{P}),\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{Q}),\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1}),\ldots,\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1}))
, and this GCD divides
d
.

7.

Sdeg_{A}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})_{j}\leq 0
for
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})\geq j>\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}
,

and
Sdeg_{A}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}}=1
. Besides,

(\tilde{U}_{k+1})_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}}=c_{k+1}x\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}+1}+\tilde{c}_{k}\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}},

where
c_{k+1}\in K
and
\tilde{c}_{k}
is a HCP from
Hcpc(p)
with
Sdeg_{A}(\tilde{c}_{k})\leq 0
and
Sdeg_{B}(\tilde{c}_{k})=-\infty
.

8.

M_{k+1}:=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})-(\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k})<M_{k}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of lemma
4.2
, with minimal modifications. For convenience of the reader we describe details below.

Item
1. follows from the property 1. for operators
\tilde{U}_{k}
,
\tilde{P}
.

Item
2. We have

[\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{k+1}]=[\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{k}]\tilde{U}_{k}^{n_{k}-1}+\tilde{U}_{k}[\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{k}]\tilde{U}_{k}^{n_{k}-2}+\ldots+\tilde{U}_{k}^{n_{k}-1}[\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{k}].

Since
[\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{k}]
and
\tilde{U}_{k}
satisfy condition
A_{p}(0)
, all terms in this sum satisfy condition
A_{p}(0)
by lemma
2.8
. Besides, all terms have the same order, because the product of symbols of
\tilde{U}_{k}
and
[\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{k}]
is not zero, as
\sigma(\tilde{U}_{k})=\varepsilon_{k}\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})}
and
\sigma([\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{k}])=\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}[\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{k}]}
(for some
\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}\in K^{*}
). Therefore, first three conditions from definition
2.9
are satisfied. Obviously,
\sigma([\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{k+1}])=n_{k}\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}\varepsilon_{k}^{n_{k}-1}\partial^{(n_{k}-1)\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})+\mathop{\rm\bf ord}[\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{k}]}
, so the last condition holds, and
[\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{k+1}]
satisfies condition
A_{p}(0)
.

Besides, by the property 6 of the operator
[\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{k}]
we get

\mathop{\rm\bf ord}([\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{k}])=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k-1})n_{k-1}-M_{k-1}+p,

whence

\mathop{\rm\bf ord}([\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{k+1}])=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})(n_{k}-1)+\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k-1})n_{k-1}-M_{k-1}+p=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}+p.

Items
3.,4. From item 1. we immediately get
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})\geq\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}
. Assume that
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})\leq\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}+1
, i.e.
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}+1
or
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}
.

1). By item 1. we know that
Sdeg_{A}([\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{k+1}]_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}+p-t})<t
for all
t>0
. Therefore,

Sdeg_{A}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}-t}<t+1

for all
t>0
.

2). Put
U_{k+1}^{\prime}:=S^{-1}\tilde{U}_{k+1}S
. Note that
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(U_{k+1}^{\prime})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})
. In the first case (i.e.
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}+1
) let’s prove that

(U_{k+1}^{\prime})_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}+1}=(\tilde{U}_{k+1})_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}+1}=\varepsilon_{k+1}\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}+1},

where
0\neq\varepsilon_{k+1}\in K
. Indeed, for all
j>\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}
we have

(\tilde{U}_{k}^{n_{k}})_{j}=\sum_{i_{1}+\ldots+i_{n_{k}}=j}(\tilde{U}_{k})_{i_{1}}\cdots(\tilde{U}_{k})_{i_{n_{k}}},

(4.8)

where
i_{q}\leq\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})
. Since
j>\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}
, we have
i_{q}>\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k-1})n_{k-1}-M_{k-1}
for all
q
. Therefore,
Sdeg_{A}((\tilde{U}_{k})_{i_{q}})\leq 0
for all
q
by property 6, and by lemma
2.6
we get
Sdeg_{A}((\tilde{U}_{k}^{n_{k}})_{j})\leq 0
for all
j>\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}
.
Therefore
Sdeg_{A}((\tilde{U}_{k+1})_{j})\leq 0
for all
j>\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}
too. Since
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}+1
, we must have
Sdeg_{A}((\tilde{U}_{k+1})_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}+1})=0
.

Now note that

(\tilde{U}_{k+1})_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}+1}=(\tilde{U}_{k}^{n_{k}})_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}+1}=(U_{k+1}^{\prime})_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}+1}\in D_{1}
(because
S^{-1}_{0}=S_{0}=1
), i.e. it is a differential operator, and by lemmas
2.1
,
2.3
this HCP does not depend on
A_{p;i}
, i.e. it looks like
\varepsilon_{k+1}\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}+1}
for
\varepsilon_{k+1}\in K^{*}
.

3). For convenience denote
\tilde{M}_{k}:=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}
. By lemma
4.1
we have

(\tilde{U}_{k+1})_{\tilde{M}_{k}}=(\tilde{U}_{k}^{n_{k}})_{\tilde{M}_{k}}=\varepsilon^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})(n_{k}-1)}((\tilde{U}_{k})_{\tilde{M}_{k-1}}\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})(n_{k}-1)}+\\
\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})}(\tilde{U}_{k})_{\tilde{M}_{k-1}}\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})(n_{k}-2)}+\ldots+\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})(n_{k}-1)}(\tilde{U}_{k})_{\tilde{M}_{k-1}})+\\
\sum_{i_{1}+\ldots+i_{n_{k}}=\tilde{M}_{k}\atop i_{1},\ldots,i_{n_{k}}>\tilde{M}_{k-1}}(\tilde{U}_{k})_{i_{1}}\cdots(\tilde{U}_{k})_{i_{n_{k}}}=n_{k}\varepsilon^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})(n_{k}-1)}c_{k}x\partial^{\tilde{M}_{k}+1}+\tilde{c}_{k}\partial^{\tilde{M}_{k}}+\\
\sum_{i_{1}+\ldots+i_{n_{k}}=\tilde{M}_{k}\atop i_{1},\ldots,i_{n_{k}}>\tilde{M}_{k-1}}(\tilde{U}_{k})_{i_{1}}\cdots(\tilde{U}_{k})_{i_{n_{k}}},

(4.9)

where
\tilde{c}_{k}
is a HCP from
Hcpc(p)
with
Sdeg_{A}(\tilde{c}_{k})\leq 0
and
Sdeg_{B}(\tilde{c}_{k})=-\infty
, because the property 6 for
\tilde{U}_{k}
holds and the HCP
(\tilde{U}_{k}^{n_{k}})_{\tilde{M}_{k}}
is totally free of
B_{j}
. Note that all terms in the last raw of this formula have
Sdeg_{A}\leq 0
by lemma
2.6
.

4). Denote
c_{k+1}:=n_{k}\varepsilon_{k}^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})(n_{k}-1)}c_{k}
. Put
\tilde{\tilde{U}}_{k+1}:=\tilde{U}_{k+1}-c_{k+1}x\partial^{\tilde{M}_{k}+1}
. In the first case (i.e.
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})=\tilde{M}_{k}+1
) this operator satisfies condition
A_{p}(0)
. Indeed, conditions 1,2 from definition
2.9
are obvious, the symbol
\sigma(\tilde{\tilde{U}}_{k+1})
does not contain
A_{p;i}
and
Sdeg_{A}(\sigma(\tilde{\tilde{U}}_{k+1}))=0
by 2), and
Sdeg_{A}((\tilde{\tilde{U}}_{k+1})_{\tilde{M}_{k}+1-t})<t
for all
t>0
by 3), 1).

Then

U_{k+1}^{\prime}=S^{-1}\tilde{\tilde{U}}_{k+1}S+c_{k+1}S^{-1}x\partial^{\tilde{M}_{k}+1}S.

By lemma
2.8
the operator
S^{-1}\tilde{\tilde{U}}_{k+1}S
satisfies condition
A_{p}(0)
and the operator
S^{-1}x\partial^{\tilde{M}_{k}+1}S
satisfies condition
A_{p}(1)
. Therefore, by lemma
2.4

Sdeg_{A}(U_{k+1}^{\prime})_{\tilde{M}_{k}-t}<t+1

for all
t>0
, and therefore

\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(U_{k+1}^{\prime})_{\tilde{M}_{k}-t}=\tilde{M}_{k}-t+Sdeg_{A}(U_{k+1}^{\prime})_{\tilde{M}_{k}-t}<\tilde{M}_{k}+1

for all
t>0
. On the other hand, since
(S^{-1})_{-1}=S_{-1}=0
and
(S^{-1})_{0}=S_{0}=1
, we have

(U_{k+1}^{\prime})_{\tilde{M}_{k}}=c_{k+1}x\partial^{\tilde{M}_{k}+1}+(\tilde{\tilde{U}}_{k+1})_{\tilde{M}_{k}},

and therefore

U_{k+1}^{\prime}=a_{\tilde{M}_{k}+1}\partial^{\tilde{M}_{k}+1}+a_{\tilde{M}_{k}}\partial^{\tilde{M}_{k}}+\ldots+a_{0}

with
a_{\tilde{M}_{k}+1}=\varepsilon_{k+1}+c_{k+1}x
and
a_{i}\in\hat{R}
for
i<\tilde{M}_{k}+1
.

5). In the second case (i.e.
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})=\tilde{M}_{k}
) by steps 1), 3) we get for all
t\geq 0

Sdeg_{A}(\tilde{\tilde{U}}_{k+1})_{\tilde{M}_{k}-t}<t+1.

Consider
U_{k+1}^{*}:=\tilde{\tilde{U}}_{k+1}+\partial^{\tilde{M}_{k}+1}
. Then
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(U_{k+1}^{*})=\tilde{M}_{k}+1
, and
U_{k+1}^{*}
satisfies condition
A_{p}(0)
. We have
\tilde{U}_{k+1}=U_{k+1}^{*}-\partial^{\tilde{M}_{k}+1}+c_{k+1}x\partial^{\tilde{M}_{k}+1}
, hence

U_{k+1}^{\prime}=S^{-1}U_{k+1}^{*}S+c_{k+1}S^{-1}x\partial^{\tilde{M}_{k}+1}S-S^{-1}\partial^{\tilde{M}_{k}+1}S.

As in step 4), by lemma
2.8
the operators
S^{-1}U_{k+1}^{*}S
,
S^{-1}\partial^{\tilde{M}_{k}+1}S
satisfy condition
A_{p}(0)
, and the operator
S^{-1}x\partial^{\tilde{M}_{k}+1}S
satisfies condition
A_{p}(1)
.
Therefore, by lemma
2.4

Sdeg_{A}(U_{k+1}^{\prime})_{\tilde{M}_{k}-t}<t+1
for any
t>0
, and therefore for any
t>0

\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(U_{k+1}^{\prime})_{\tilde{M}_{k}-t}=\tilde{M}_{k}-t+Sdeg_{A}(U_{k+1}^{\prime})_{\tilde{M}_{k}-t}<\tilde{M}_{k}+1.

On the other hand, since
(S^{-1})_{-1}=S_{-1}=0
and
(S^{-1})_{0}=S_{0}=1
, we have

(U_{k+1}^{\prime})_{\tilde{M}_{k}}=c_{k+1}x\partial^{\tilde{M}_{k}+1}+(\tilde{\tilde{U}}_{k+1})_{\tilde{M}_{k}}

and therefore
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(U_{k+1}^{\prime})_{\tilde{M}_{k}}=\tilde{M}_{k}+1
(as
Sdeg_{A}(\tilde{\tilde{U}}_{k+1})_{\tilde{M}_{k}}\leq 0
) and

U_{k+1}^{\prime}=c_{k+1}x\partial^{\tilde{M}_{k}+1}+a_{\tilde{M}_{k}}\partial^{\tilde{M}_{k}}+\ldots+a_{0}

with
a_{i}\in\hat{R}
for
i<\tilde{M}_{k}+1
.

6). Note that
\Phi_{u}^{-1}(U_{k+1}^{\prime})\in A_{1}
. Then in the second case (i.e.
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})=\tilde{M}_{k}
) the highest coefficient of the differential operator
\Phi_{u}^{-1}(U_{k+1}^{\prime})
will be equal to
H:=c_{k+1}fg^{(\tilde{M}_{k}+1)/d}\in x\cdot K[x]
, and in the first case (i.e.
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})=\tilde{M}_{k}+1
) it will be equal to
\tilde{H}:=(\varepsilon_{k+1}+c_{k+1}f)g^{(\tilde{M}_{k}+1)/d}\in K[x]
. So, either
c_{k+1}f=Hg^{-(\tilde{M}_{k}+1)/d}
or

\varepsilon_{k+1}+c_{k+1}f=\tilde{H}g^{-(\tilde{M}_{k}+1)/d}
. In both cases, differentiating these equalities with respect to
x
we get (we’ll use again the same notation
H
both for
H
and
\tilde{H}
below)

c_{k+1}f^{\prime}=c_{k+1}g^{-1/d}=H^{\prime}g^{-(\tilde{M}_{k}+1)/d}-\frac{\tilde{M}_{k}+1}{d}Hg^{-1-(\tilde{M}_{k}+1)/d}g^{\prime},

whence

c_{k+1}g^{1+\frac{\tilde{M}_{k}}{d}}=H^{\prime}g-\frac{\tilde{M}_{k}+1}{d}Hg^{\prime}

(4.10)

(obviously,
d_{2}|\tilde{M}_{k}
, but
d
may not divide
\tilde{M}_{k}
; so, in particular, in this case all multiplicities of
g
are divisible by a divisor
d_{1}
of
GCD(d,l)
, i.e.
g=\tilde{g}^{d_{1}}
,
\tilde{g}\in{\mbox{\dbl C}}[x]
,
d=d_{1}d_{3}
and
d_{3}|\tilde{M}_{k}
). Just the same arguments as in lemma
4.2
, 3. 6) show that
\deg_{x}(H)=(1+\frac{\tilde{M}_{k}}{d})l-(l-1)
20
20

20

Note again that if
l=d
, these arguments imply that (
4.10
) is impossible,
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})>\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})n_{k}-M_{k}+1
.
.

Dividing the equation (
4.10
) by
\tilde{g}^{d_{1}-1}
, we get the equation

c_{k+1}\tilde{g}^{1+\frac{\tilde{M}_{k}}{d_{3}}}=H^{\prime}\tilde{g}-\frac{\tilde{M}_{k}+1}{d_{3}}H\tilde{g}^{\prime},

and by lemma
4.3
(for
A=1+\frac{\tilde{M}_{k}}{d_{3}}
,
\tilde{d}=d_{3}
,
\tilde{l}=l/d_{1}
,
\tilde{z}=\tilde{M}_{k}
) this equation has a solution only if
\tilde{g}
has one root, because
l/d<1
.

7). If
g
has only one root, then
g=(1+\alpha x)^{l}
for some
\alpha\in K
. In this case necessarily
H=\tilde{c}(1+\alpha x)^{(1+\frac{\tilde{M}_{k}}{d})l-l+1}
,
\tilde{c}\in K
. But in the second case
H(0)=0
- a contradiction, so item 2) holds. So, only the first case is possible.

Item
5. By the same arguments as in item 2. 2) we get
(U_{k+1}^{\prime})_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(U_{k+1}^{\prime})}=(\tilde{U}_{k}^{n})_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(U_{k+1}^{\prime})}=(\tilde{U}_{k+1})_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})}\in D_{1}
and therefore
(U_{k+1}^{\prime})_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(U_{k+1}^{\prime})}=\varepsilon_{k+1}\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})}
with
0\neq\varepsilon_{k+1}\in K
, because
Sdeg_{A}((\tilde{U}_{k+1})_{j})\leq 0
for all
j>\tilde{M}_{k}
(see (
4.8
) and explanations in 3. 2).).

By item 2. we know that
Sdeg_{A}([\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{k+1}]_{\tilde{M}_{k}+p-t})<t
for all
t>0
. Therefore,

Sdeg_{A}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})_{\tilde{M}_{k}-t}<t+1

for all
t>0
. Since
\chi:=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})>\tilde{M}_{k}+1
, we get

Sdeg_{A}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})_{\chi-t}<t

for all
t>0
. In particular, the operator
\tilde{U}_{k+1}
satisfies condition
A_{p}(0)
.

Item
6. Since the operator
\tilde{U}_{k+1}
satisfies condition
A_{p}(0)
, the operator
U_{k+1}^{\prime}
also satisfies condition
A_{p}(0)
by proposition
2.5
. Therefore,
U_{k+1}^{\prime}
is a formally elliptic differential operator (cf. similar arguments in 3., 4),5)), i.e.
U_{k+1}^{\prime}=\varepsilon_{k+1}\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})}+\ldots
, and therefore,
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(U_{k+1}^{\prime})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(U_{k+1}^{\prime})
.

Analogously,

[P^{\prime},U_{k+1}^{\prime}]=S^{-1}[\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{k+1}]S\in D_{1}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathop{\rm\bf ord}([P^{\prime},U_{k+1}^{\prime}])=\tilde{M}_{k}+p.

By proposition
2.5

[P^{\prime},U_{k+1}^{\prime}]
satisfies condition
A_{p}(0)
. Since
U_{k+1}^{\prime},[P^{\prime},U_{k+1}^{\prime}]
are differential operators, their homogeneous components are also differential operators, i.e. they don’t depend on
A_{p,i}
, cf. lemma
2.3
. Then, for any
t>0
we have

\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits([P^{\prime},U_{k+1}^{\prime}]_{\tilde{M}_{k}+p-t})=Sdeg_{A}([P^{\prime},U_{k+1}^{\prime}]_{\tilde{M}_{k}+p-t})+\tilde{M}_{k}+p-t<\tilde{M}_{k}+p,

and
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits([P^{\prime},U_{k+1}^{\prime}]_{\tilde{M}_{k}+p})=\tilde{M}_{k}+p=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}([P^{\prime},U_{k+1}^{\prime}])
, i.e. the highest term of the
differential operator

[P^{\prime},U_{k+1}^{\prime}]
coincides with
[P^{\prime},U_{k+1}^{\prime}]_{\tilde{M}_{k}+p}=n_{k}\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}\varepsilon_{k}^{n_{k}-1}\partial^{(n_{k}-1)\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})+\mathop{\rm\bf ord}[\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{k}]}
, see item 2.

Put
Q_{k+1}:=\Phi_{u}^{-1}(U_{k+1}^{\prime})
. Note that
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{k+1})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(Q_{k+1})=\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(U_{k+1}^{\prime})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(U_{k+1}^{\prime})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})
, because
\Phi_{u}^{-1}
preserves the order filtration on
D_{1}
and
g(0)\neq 0
. Now we have

\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits([P,Q_{k+1}])=\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits\Phi_{u}^{-1}([P^{\prime},U_{k+1}^{\prime}])=\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits([P^{\prime},U_{k+1}^{\prime}])=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}([P^{\prime},U_{k+1}^{\prime}])=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}([\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{k+1}]).

Since by items 3., 2. we have
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(P)+\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{k+1})-1>\tilde{M}_{k}+p=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}([\tilde{P},\tilde{U}_{k+1}])
, it follows by
[
13
, L. 2.7]
that
f_{0,1}(P)
is proportional with
f_{0,1}(Q_{k+1})
, i.e.
f_{0,1}(Q_{k+1})^{p}=\tilde{c}f_{0,1}(P)^{\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{k+1})}=\tilde{c}f_{0,1}(P)^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})}
. Therefore,
l\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k+1})
is divisible by
d
, i.e.
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{Q}_{k+1})
is divisible by
d_{2}
.

Items
7., 8. immediately follow from item 3., 4. (cf. formulae (
4.8
), (
4.9
)).

∎

If
g
has more than one root, then by induction lemma
4.4
we get a sequence of operators
\tilde{U}_{k}
satisfying properties 1-8 from lemma. But the sequence
M_{k}
strictly decreases, and at the same time
M_{k}>0
by property 3, a contradiction.
21
21

21

The same contradiction proves that
l\neq d
(i.e.
l<d
). Thus we get as a byproduct an alternative proof of
[
21
, Th. 4.1 (3)]
.

If
g
has one root, then the sequence of operators
\tilde{U}_{k}
can stop, by item 4., on an operator
\tilde{U}_{I}
with
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{I})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{I-1})n_{I-1}-M_{I-1}+1
. Besides, we have the sequence of differential operators
U_{k}^{\prime}\in D_{1}
and
Q_{k}\in A_{1}
. From the proof of item 6. we know for
k=1,\ldots,I-1
that

\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{k})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(Q_{k})=\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(U_{k}^{\prime})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(U_{k}^{\prime})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{k})

and therefore
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{I-1})n_{I-1}-M_{I-1}
is divisible by
d_{2}
, so
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{I})=1
mod
d_{2}
. Moreover, since
d|M_{0}
, by induction we get that

GCD\{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{P}),\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{Q}),\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1}),\ldots,\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{I-1})\}
divides
M_{I-1}
, hence
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{I-1})n_{I-1}-M_{I-1}
is divisible by this GCD, and therefore

\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{I})=1\quad\mbox{mod}\quad GCD\{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{P}),\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{Q}),\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{1}),\ldots,\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{I-1})\}.

By item 4. we also know that

U_{I}^{\prime}=a_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{I-1})n_{I-1}-M_{I-1}+1}\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{I-1})n_{I-1}-M_{I-1}+1}+a_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{I-1})n_{I-1}-M_{I-1}}\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{I-1})n_{I-1}-M_{I-1}}+\ldots+a_{0}

with
a_{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{I-1})n_{I-1}-M_{I-1}+1}=\varepsilon_{k+1}+c_{k+1}x
and
a_{i}\in\hat{R}
for
i<\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{I-1})n_{I-1}-M_{I-1}+1
. Then
Q_{I}:=\Phi_{u}^{-1}(U_{I}^{\prime})\in A_{1}
and

\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{I})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{U}_{I-1})n_{I-1}-M_{I-1}+1,

because
\Phi_{u}^{-1}
preserves the order filtration on
D_{1}
and
g(0)\neq 0
.

So, the sequence of operators
Q_{i}
is the needed sequence
F_{i}(P,Q)
from theorem.

The equality (
4.1
) follows from

\sum_{i=0}^{I-1}(n_{i}\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits F_{i}(P,Q)-\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits F_{i+1}(P,Q))=\sum_{i=0}^{I-1}(M_{i}-M_{i+1})=M_{0}-M_{I}=p+q-1.

∎

We can say more about the operators
F_{i}(P,Q)
from theorem
4.1
. Namely, we’ll show below that they are of subrectangular type. Before we prove it, let’s make several preliminary comments.

Assume there exists a DC-pair
P,Q\in A_{1}
that provides a counterexample to the Dixmier conjecture and
\varphi
is the corresponding endomorphism with
\varphi(x)=P
,
\varphi(\partial)=Q
. Then, as it was noted in section
3
there exists a DC-pair of subrectangular type satisfying conditions of theorem
4.1
and conditions of lemma
3.2
(remark
3.2
). Moreover, without loss of generality and by remark
4.1
we can assume

f_{0,1}(P)=(1+\alpha x)^{ln}y^{dn},\quad f_{0,1}(Q)=(1+\alpha x)^{lm}y^{dm},\quad 1<l<d,\quad(m,n)=1,\quad\epsilon(\varphi)\notin{\mbox{\dbl N}}.

Let
f_{1,0}(P)=\tilde{h}x^{ln}
,
\tilde{h}\in{\mbox{\dbl C}}[y]
, then
\tilde{h}=\tilde{g}^{n}
for some
\tilde{g}\in{\mbox{\dbl C}}[y]
of degree
d
and
f_{1,0}(Q)=\tilde{g}^{m}x^{lm}
(cf. arguments in section
3
).

For convenience, let’s introduce one notation (from
[
21
]
): for an operator
B\in A_{1}
with
f_{1,0}(B)=hx^{v_{1,0}(B)}
,
h\in{\mbox{\dbl C}}[y]
,
f_{0,1}(B)=gy^{v_{0,1}(B)}
,
g\in{\mbox{\dbl C}}[x]
, we define

en_{1,0}(B):=(v_{1,0}(B),\deg_{y}(h(y))),\quad st_{1,0}(B):=(v_{1,0}(B),-\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(h(x))),

en_{0,1}(B):=(-\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(g(x)),v_{0,1}(B)),\quad st_{0,1}(B):=(\deg_{x}(g(x)),v_{0,1}(B)).

Theorem 4.2
.

Under assumptions of theorem
4.1
, the operators
F_{i}(P,Q)
,
i=0,\ldots,I
are of subrectangular type.

Let
f_{1,0}(Q)=\tilde{g}^{m}x^{lm}
,
f_{1,0}(Q_{I})=H_{1}x^{v_{1,0}(Q_{I})}
, where
\tilde{g}=\tilde{g}_{1}^{d_{1}}
,
d=d_{1}d_{3}
,
l=d_{1}l_{3}
,
H_{1}\in K[y]
. If the polynomial
\tilde{g}
has one root, say
\tilde{g}=(b+\tilde{\alpha}y)^{d}
,
22
22

22

\tilde{\alpha}^{d}=\alpha^{l}
then
H_{1}=c(b+\tilde{\alpha}y)^{v_{0,1}(Q_{I})}
for some
c\in K
.

If the polynomial
\tilde{g}
has more than one root, then
H_{1}
satisfies the differential equation

\frac{\partial H_{1}}{\partial y}\tilde{g}_{1}-\frac{(l(M-n)+1)n_{I-1}}{l_{3}}H_{1}\frac{\partial\tilde{g}_{1}}{\partial y}=\tilde{c}_{1}H^{n_{I-1}-1}\tilde{g}_{1}^{d_{1}(M-n)+1},\quad\tilde{c}_{1}\in K,

(4.11)

where
H\in K[y]
,
f_{1,0}(Q_{I-1})=Hx^{v_{1,0}(Q_{I-1})}
, satisfies the differential equation

\frac{\partial H}{\partial y}\tilde{g}_{1}-\frac{l(M-n)+1}{l_{3}}H\frac{\partial\tilde{g}_{1}}{\partial y}=\tilde{c}\tilde{g}_{1}^{d_{1}(M-n)+1},\quad\tilde{c}\in K,

(4.12)

and

M=\frac{(n_{I-2}-1)\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{I-2})+(n_{I-3}-1)\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{I-3})+\ldots+(n-1)dm}{d}

(note that
H_{1},H
are uniquely determined by equations (
4.11
), (
4.12
)).

Moreover, the polynomial
\tilde{g}
has more that one root only if
l|d
, and in this case

\deg H_{1}=((l(M-n)+1)n_{I-1}-1)d_{2}+1,\quad\deg H=(l(M-n)+1)d_{2}.

Proof.

The proof is by induction. For
F_{0}(P,Q)
the claim is trivial.

For
i=1
we have the following alternative:

Case
1. We have
\{f_{1,0}(Q_{1}),f_{1,0}(P)\}=0
.

Then by theorem
2.2
we have
f_{1,0}(Q_{1})^{ln}=cf_{1,0}(P)^{v_{1,0}(Q_{1})}
, i.e.
f_{1,0}(Q_{1})=c\tilde{g}^{N}x^{v_{1,0}(Q_{1})}
, where
N=v_{1,0}(Q_{1})/l
.
So,
en_{1,0}(Q_{1})=(l,d)v_{1,0}(Q_{1})/l
.
By lemma
4.2
we have two possible cases:

a) if
v_{0,1}(Q_{1})=\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{1})>dm(n-1)-dn+1
, then
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{1})
is divisible by
d_{2}
and
f_{0,1}(Q_{1})^{dn}=\tilde{c}f_{0,1}(P)^{\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{1})}
(see the proof of item 6 in lemma). So, in particular,

st_{0,1}(Q_{1})dn=st_{0,1}(P)\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{1})
, i.e.
st_{0,1}(Q_{1})=(l,d)\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{1})/d
and
st_{0,1}(Q_{1})
,
en_{1,0}(Q_{1})
are proportional. Since
v_{1,0}(Q_{1})\geq l\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{1})/d
and
dv_{1,0}(Q_{1})/l\leq\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{1})
, we must have
v_{1,0}(Q_{1})=l\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{1})/d
and therefore
st_{0,1}(Q_{1})=en_{1,0}(Q_{1})
, i.e.
Q_{1}
is of subrectangular type. Moreover, in this case
f_{0,1}(Q_{1})
is a (fractional) power of
f_{0,1}(P)
and

f_{1,0}(Q_{1})
is a (fractional) power of
f_{1,0}(P)
, i.e.
st_{0,1}(Q_{1})
is proportional with
st_{0,1}(P)
and
en_{1,0}(Q_{1})
is proportional with
en_{1,0}(P)=st_{0,1}(P)
.

b) if
v_{0,1}(Q_{1})=\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{1})=dm(n-1)-dn+1
, then
st_{0,1}(Q_{1})=(l(mn-m-n)+1,d(mn-m-n)+1)
(see the proof of item 8 in lemma
4.2
). But we have
v_{1,0}(Q_{1})\geq l(mn-m-n)+1
and
d(mn-m-n)+1\geq dv_{1,0}(Q_{1})/l
, whence
l/d\geq 1
- a contradiction, as
l<d
. So, this case is impossible.

Case
2. We have
\{f_{1,0}(Q_{1}),f_{1,0}(P)\}\neq 0
. Consider the following two cases:

a)
\tilde{g}
has more than one root.

Since
[Q,P]=1
, by theorem
2.2
we have
\{f_{1,0}(Q),f_{1,0}(P)\}=0
. Recall that in step 2 of the proof of lemma
4.2
we get

[P,Q_{1}]=-nQ^{n-1}
. So, the operator
[P,Q_{1}]
is of subrectangular type, and by theorem
2.2
we have
f_{1,0}([Q_{1},P])=nf_{1,0}(Q)^{n-1}=n\tilde{g}^{m(n-1)}x^{lm(n-1)}
.

Then by theorem
2.2
we have

\{f_{1,0}(Q_{1}),f_{1,0}(P)\}=f_{1,0}([Q_{1},P]),\quad v_{1,0}(Q_{1})+v_{1,0}(P)-1=v_{1,0}([Q_{1},P])=(n-1)lm.

Let
f_{1,0}(Q_{1})=Hx^{v_{1,0}(Q_{1})}
,
H\in K[y]
. Then

\{f_{1,0}(Q_{1}),f_{1,0}(P)\}=-ln\frac{\partial H}{\partial y}\tilde{g}^{n}x^{v_{1,0}(Q_{1})+ln-1}+nv_{1,0}(Q_{1})H\tilde{g}^{n-1}\frac{\partial\tilde{g}}{\partial y}x^{v_{1,0}(Q_{1})+ln-1},

so we get the equation

-l\frac{\partial H}{\partial y}\tilde{g}^{n}x^{v_{1,0}(Q_{1})+ln-1}+v_{1,0}(Q_{1})H\tilde{g}^{n-1}\frac{\partial\tilde{g}}{\partial y}x^{v_{1,0}(Q_{1})+ln-1}=\tilde{g}^{m(n-1)}x^{lm(n-1)},

whence
v_{1,0}(Q_{1})=lm(n-1)-ln+1
and

-l\frac{\partial H}{\partial y}\tilde{g}+v_{1,0}(Q_{1})H\frac{\partial\tilde{g}}{\partial y}=\tilde{g}^{(m-1)(n-1)}.

(4.13)

By lemma
4.3
(for
A=(m-1)(n-1)
,
\tilde{d}=l
,
\tilde{l}=d
,
\tilde{z}=l(mn-m-n)
) we have
\deg_{y}(H)=d(mn-m-n)+\frac{d}{l}
(note that it is possible only if
d/l=d_{2}
). Hence,
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{1})>d(mn-m-n)+1
and then by lemma
4.2

\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{1})
is divisible by
d_{2}
and
st_{0,1}(Q_{1})
is proportional with
st_{0,1}(P)=(ln,dn)
, i.e.
st_{0,1}(Q_{1})=(l\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{1})/d,\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{1}))
. On the other hand, we have in this case also

en_{1,0}(Q_{1})=(l(mn-m-n)+1,d(mn-m-n)+d_{2})

and therefore
en_{1,0}(Q_{1})
is also proportional with
st_{0,1}(P)=(ln,dn)\sim(1,d_{2})
. But then
st_{0,1}(Q_{1})=en_{1,0}(Q_{1})
(as
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{1})\geq d(mn-m-n)+d_{2}
and
l(mn-m-n)+1\geq l\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{1})/d
), and
Q_{1}
is of subrectangular type.

Now note that
M_{1}=d_{2}
(in the notation of lemma
4.2
). So,
0<M_{2}<d_{2}
and therefore
M_{2}
can not be divisible by
d_{2}
, hence by lemma
4.4

M_{2}=1
. This means that
I=2
, and
Q_{2}
is the last operator in the sequence for
P,Q
. By item 3.4.7) of lemma
4.4
we have in this case
n_{1}=ln
,
\tilde{M}_{1}=(d(mn-m-n)+d_{2})n_{1}-d_{2}
,

\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{2})=\tilde{M}_{1}+1
and

st_{0,1}(Q_{2})=((\frac{(d(mn-m-n)+d_{2})n_{1}-d_{2}}{d})l+1,(d(mn-m-n)+d_{2})n_{1}-d_{2}+1)=\\
((l(mn-m-n)+1)n_{1},((l(mn-m-n)+1)n_{1}-1)d_{2}+1).

So,
st_{0,1}(Q_{2})
is not proportional with
st_{0,1}(P)=(ln,dn)\sim(1,d_{2})
. Now we have again two cases:

aa)
\{f_{1,0}(Q_{2}),f_{1,0}(P)\}=0
. Then by theorem
2.2
we have
en_{1,0}(Q_{2})
is proportional with
en_{1,0}(P)=(ln,dn)\sim(1,d_{2})
. Let, say,
en_{1,0}(Q_{2})=(A,Ad_{2})
. Then we have

((l(mn-m-n)+1)n_{1}-1)d_{2}+1\geq Ad_{2}),\quad A\geq(l(mn-m-n)+1)n_{1},

whence
-d_{2}+1\geq 0
- a contradiction. So, this case is impossible.

ab)
\{f_{1,0}(Q_{2}),f_{1,0}(P)\}\neq 0
. Then by theorem
2.2
we have
\{f_{1,0}(Q_{2}),f_{1,0}(P)\}=f_{1,0}([Q_{2},P])
. Note that

[Q_{2},P]=[Q_{1},P]Q_{1}^{n_{1}-1}+Q_{1}[Q_{1},P]Q_{1}^{n_{1}-2}+\ldots+Q_{1}^{n_{1}-1}[Q_{1},P]=\\
nQ^{n-1}Q_{1}^{n_{1}-1}+nQ_{1}Q^{n-1}Q_{1}^{n_{1}-2}+\ldots+nQ_{1}^{n_{1}-1}Q^{n-1},

and by remark
3.1
the operator
[Q_{2},P]
is of subrectangular type (as all summands are of subrectangular type with the same highest monomials). In particular,
v_{1,0}([Q_{2},P])=(n_{1}-1)v_{1,0}(Q_{1})+(n-1)lm
.
Moreover, by theorem
2.2
we also have

v_{1,0}(Q_{2})+v_{1,0}(P)-1=v_{1,0}([Q_{2},P])=(n_{1}-1)v_{1,0}(Q_{1})+(n-1)lm=(n_{1}-1)(l(mn-m-n)+1)+(n-1)lm,

hence

v_{1,0}(Q_{2})=(l(mn-m-n)+1)n_{1},

and the vertex
st_{0,1}(Q_{2})
belong to the
(1,0)
-top line of
Q_{2}
(as it is vertical and passes through the point
((l(mn-m-n)+1)n_{1},0)
). But then
en_{1,0}(Q_{2})=st_{0,1}(Q_{2})
and
Q_{2}
is of subrectangular type.

b)
\tilde{g}
has one root.

Then
\tilde{g}=(b+\tilde{\alpha}y)^{d}
for some
b\in K
. In this case the solution of the equation (
4.13
) is of the form
H=c(b+\tilde{\alpha}y)^{d(mn-m-n)+1}
, hence
en_{1,0}(Q_{1})=(l(mn-m-n)+1,d(mn-m-n)+1)
. According to lemma
4.2
we have two possibilities:

ba)
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{1})
is divisible by
d_{2}
, and therefore
st_{0,1}(Q_{1})
is proportional with
st_{0,1}(P)=(ln,dn)
(see the proof of item 6 in lemma). Let, say,
st_{0,1}(Q_{1})=(lA,dA)
. Then we have

l(mn-m-n)+1\geq lA
and
dA\geq d(mn-m-n)+1
, whence

l(mn-m-n)+1\geq lA\geq l(mn-m-n)+l/d.

Since
l<d
, we must have
lA=l(mn-m-n)+1
, hence
dA=d(mn-m-n)+d/l
. Again it is possible only if
d/l=d_{2}
. But then the vertex
st_{0,1}(Q_{1})
belongs to the
(1,0)
-top line of
Q_{1}
, and lies above the vertex
en_{1,0}(Q_{1})
– a contradiction. So, this case is impossible.

bb)
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{1})
is not divisible by
d_{2}
. Then
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{1})=d(mn-m-n)+1
, and
st_{0,1}(Q_{1})=(l(mn-m-n)+1,d(mn-m-n)+1)
(see item 3.4. 8) of lemma). So,
st_{0,1}(Q_{1})=en_{1,0}(Q_{1})
and therefore
Q_{1}
is of subrectangular type. Besides, in this case
I=1
.

Summing up, note that only in the case 1 a) we need to continue our research in order to prove the assertion. So, continuing this line of reasoning, by induction we can assume for
1<i<I
that
Q_{i}
is an operator such that
[Q_{i},P]
is of subrectangular type and that
f_{0,1}([Q_{i},P])
is a (fractional) power of
f_{0,1}(P)
and

f_{1,0}([Q_{i},P])
is a (fractional) power of
f_{1,0}(P)
(cf. item 1 a)). Indeed, this follows from the induction hypothesis, remark
3.1
, theorem
2.2
, and since

[Q_{i},P]=[Q_{i-1},P]Q_{i-1}^{n_{i-1}-1}+Q_{i-1}[Q_{i-1},P]Q_{i-1}^{n_{i-1}-2}+\ldots+Q_{i-1}^{n_{i-1}-1}[Q_{i-1},P].

In particular,
st_{0,1}([Q_{i},P])
is proportional with
st_{0,1}(P)=(ln,dn)
and
en_{1,0}([Q_{i},P])
is proportional with
en_{1,0}(P)=st_{0,1}(P)=(ln,dn)
.

For this induction step we need to repeat all arguments given above for the operator
Q_{1}
. They are practically the same. For convenience of the reader we give them here.

Case
1. We have
\{f_{1,0}(Q_{i}),f_{1,0}(P)\}=0
. Then by theorem
2.2
we have
f_{1,0}(Q_{i})^{ln}=cf_{1,0}(P)^{v_{1,0}(Q_{i})}
, i.e.
f_{1,0}(Q_{i})=c\tilde{g}^{N_{i}}x^{v_{1,0}(Q_{i})}
, where
N_{i}=v_{1,0}(Q_{i})/l
.
So,
en_{1,0}(Q_{i})=(l,d)v_{1,0}(Q_{i})/l
.
By lemma
4.4
we have two possible cases:

a) if
v_{0,1}(Q_{i})=\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i})>\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i-1})n_{i-1}-M_{i-1}+1
, then
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i})
is divisible by
d_{2}
and
f_{0,1}(Q_{i})^{dn}=\tilde{c}f_{0,1}(P)^{\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i})}
(see the proof of item 6 in lemma
4.4
). So, in particular,

st_{0,1}(Q_{i})dn=st_{0,1}(P)\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i})
, i.e.
st_{0,1}(Q_{i})=(l,d)\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i})/d
and
st_{0,1}(Q_{i})
,
en_{1,0}(Q_{i})
are proportional. Since
v_{1,0}(Q_{i})\geq l\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i})/d
and
dv_{1,0}(Q_{i})/l\leq\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i})
, we must have
v_{1,0}(Q_{i})=l\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i})/d
and therefore
st_{0,1}(Q_{i})=en_{1,0}(Q_{i})
, i.e.
Q_{i}
is of subrectangular type. Moreover, in this case
f_{0,1}(Q_{i})
is a (fractional) power of
f_{0,1}(P)
and

f_{1,0}(Q_{i})
is a (fractional) power of
f_{1,0}(P)
.

b) if
v_{0,1}(Q_{i})=\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i})=\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i-1})n_{i-1}-M_{i-1}+1=:\tilde{M}_{i}+1
, then

st_{0,1}(Q_{i})=(l\tilde{M}_{i}/d+1,\tilde{M}_{i}+1)
(see the proof of item 3.4.7) in lemma
4.4
). But we have
v_{1,0}(Q_{i})\geq l\tilde{M}_{i}/d+1
and
\tilde{M}_{i}+1\geq dv_{1,0}(Q_{i})/l
, whence
d/l\leq 1
- a contradiction, as
l<d
. So, this case is impossible.

Case
2. We have
\{f_{1,0}(Q_{i}),f_{1,0}(P)\}\neq 0
. Consider the following two cases:

a)
\tilde{g}
has more than one root.

Then by theorem
2.2
we have

\{f_{1,0}(Q_{i}),f_{1,0}(P)\}=f_{1,0}([Q_{i},P])=c_{0}\tilde{g}^{M}x^{v_{1,0}([Q_{i},P])},\quad\mbox{where}\\
v_{1,0}(Q_{i})+v_{1,0}(P)-1=v_{1,0}([Q_{i},P])=(n_{i-1}-1)v_{1,0}(Q_{i-1})+(n_{i-2}-1)v_{1,0}(Q_{i-2})+\ldots+(n-1)lm,\\
dM=(n_{i-1}-1)\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i-1})+(n_{i-2}-1)\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i-2})+\ldots+(n-1)dm,

because
[Q_{i},P]
is of subrectangular type. Since
en_{1,0}([Q_{i},P])
is proportional to
(l,d)
, we get
v_{1,0}([Q_{i},P])=lM
. Obviously,
d_{2}
divides the right hand side of the expression for
dM
(cf. the proof of item 3.4. 6) of lemma
4.4
), but
d
may not divide it; so, in particular, in this case all multiplicities of
\tilde{g}
are divisible by a divisor
d_{1}
of
GCD(d,l)
, i.e.
\tilde{g}=\tilde{g}_{1}^{d_{1}}
,
\tilde{g}_{1}\in{\mbox{\dbl C}}[x]
,
d=d_{1}d_{3}
and
d_{3}
divides the right hand side of the expression for
dM
. Moreover,
d_{1}
divides
l
,
l:=d_{1}l_{3}
.

Let
f_{1,0}(Q_{i})=Hx^{v_{1,0}(Q_{i})}
,
H\in K[y]
. Then

\{f_{1,0}(Q_{i}),f_{1,0}(P)\}=-ln\frac{\partial H}{\partial y}\tilde{g}^{n}x^{v_{1,0}(Q_{i})+ln-1}+nv_{1,0}(Q_{i})H\tilde{g}^{n-1}\frac{\partial\tilde{g}}{\partial y}x^{v_{1,0}(Q_{i})+ln-1},

so we get the equation

-l\frac{\partial H}{\partial y}\tilde{g}^{n}x^{v_{1,0}(Q_{i})+ln-1}+v_{1,0}(Q_{i})H\tilde{g}^{n-1}\frac{\partial\tilde{g}}{\partial y}x^{v_{1,0}(Q_{i})+ln-1}=c_{0}\tilde{g}^{M}x^{v_{1,0}([Q_{i},P])},

whence
v_{1,0}(Q_{i})=v_{1,0}([Q_{i},P])-ln+1
(so,
(v_{1,0}(Q_{i}),l)=1
) and

-l\frac{\partial H}{\partial y}\tilde{g}+v_{1,0}(Q_{i})H\frac{\partial\tilde{g}}{\partial y}=c\tilde{g}^{M-n+1},\quad c=c_{0}/n.

(4.14)

Dividing the equation (
4.14
) by
-l\tilde{g}_{1}^{d_{1}-1}
, we get the equation

\frac{\partial H}{\partial y}\tilde{g}_{1}-\frac{l(M-n)+1}{l_{3}}H\frac{\partial\tilde{g}_{1}}{\partial y}=\tilde{c}\tilde{g}_{1}^{d_{1}(M-n)+1},\quad\tilde{c}=-c/l.

By lemma
4.3
(for
A=d_{1}(M-n)+1
,
\tilde{d}=l_{3}
,
\tilde{l}=d/d_{1}
,
\tilde{z}=l(M-n)
) we have
\deg_{y}(H)=d(M-n)+\frac{d}{l}
(note that again it is possible only if
d/l=d_{2}
). Hence,
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i})>d_{1}(M-n)+1
and then by lemma
4.4

\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i})
is divisible by
d_{2}
and
st_{0,1}(Q_{i})
is proportional with
st_{0,1}(P)=(ln,dn)
, i.e.
st_{0,1}(Q_{i})=(l\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i})/d,\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i}))
. On the other hand, we have in this case also

en_{1,0}(Q_{i})=(l(M-n)+1,d(M-n)+d_{2})

and therefore
en_{1,0}(Q_{i})
is also proportional with
st_{0,1}(P)=(ln,dn)\sim(1,d_{2})
. But then
st_{0,1}(Q_{i})=en_{1,0}(Q_{i})
(as
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i})\geq d(M-n)+d_{2}
and
l(M-n)+1\geq l\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i})/d
), and
Q_{i}
is of subrectangular type.

Now note that
M_{i}=d_{2}
(in the notation of lemma
4.4
). So,
0<M_{i+1}<d_{2}
and therefore
M_{i+1}
can not be divisible by
d_{2}
, hence by lemma
4.4

M_{i+1}=1
. This means that
I=i+1
, and
Q_{i+1}
is the last operator in the sequence for
P,Q
. By item 3.4.7) of lemma
4.4
we can put in this case
n_{i}=dn
,
\tilde{M}_{i}=(d(M-n)+d_{2})n_{i}-d_{2}
,

\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i+1})=\tilde{M}_{i}+1
and

st_{0,1}(Q_{i+1})=((\frac{(d(M-n)+d_{2})n_{i}-d_{2}}{d})l+1,(d(M-n)+d_{2})n_{i}-d_{2}+1)=\\
((l(M-n)+1)n_{i},((l(M-n)+1)n_{i}-1)d_{2}+1).

So,
st_{0,1}(Q_{i+1})
is not proportional with
st_{0,1}(P)=(ln,dn)\sim(1,d_{2})
. Now we have again two cases:

aa)
\{f_{1,0}(Q_{i+1}),f_{1,0}(P)\}=0
. Then by theorem
2.2
we have
en_{1,0}(Q_{i+1})
is proportional with
en_{1,0}(P)=(ln,dn)\sim(1,d_{2})
. Let, say,
en_{1,0}(Q_{i+1})=(A,Ad_{2})
. Then we have

((l(M-n)+1)n_{i}-1)d_{2}+1\geq Ad_{2},\quad A\geq(l(M-n)+1)n_{i},

whence
-d_{2}+1\geq 0
- a contradiction. So, this case is impossible.

ab)
\{f_{1,0}(Q_{i+1}),f_{1,0}(P)\}\neq 0
. Then by theorem
2.2
we have

\{f_{1,0}(Q_{i+1}),f_{1,0}(P)\}=f_{1,0}([Q_{i+1},P])
. Note that

[Q_{i+1},P]=[Q_{i},P]Q_{i}^{n_{i}-1}+Q_{i}[Q_{i},P]Q_{i}^{n_{i}-2}+\ldots+Q_{i}^{n_{i}-1}[Q_{i},P],

and by remark
3.1
the operator
[Q_{i+1},P]
is of subrectangular type (as all summands are of subrectangular type with the same highest monomials by the induction hypothesis). In particular,
v_{1,0}([Q_{i+1},P])=(n_{i}-1)v_{1,0}(Q_{i})+v_{1,0}([Q_{i},P])
.
Moreover, by theorem
2.2
we also have

v_{1,0}(Q_{i+1})+v_{1,0}(P)-1=v_{1,0}([Q_{i+1},P])=(n_{i}-1)v_{1,0}(Q_{i})+v_{1,0}([Q_{i},P])=(n_{i}-1)(l(M-n)+1)+lM,

hence

v_{1,0}(Q_{i+1})=(l(M-n)+1)n_{i},

and the vertex
st_{0,1}(Q_{i+1})
belongs to the
(1,0)
-top line of
Q_{i+1}
(as it is vertical and passes through the point
((l(M-n)+1)n_{i},0)
). But then
en_{1,0}(Q_{i+1})=st_{0,1}(Q_{i+1})
and
Q_{i+1}
is of subrectangular type.

Put
f_{1,0}(Q_{i+1})=H_{1}x^{v_{1,0}(Q_{i+1})}
,
H_{1}\in K[y]
. Repeating calculations as above, we get another differential equation on
H_{1}
:

\frac{\partial H_{1}}{\partial y}\tilde{g}_{1}-\frac{(l(M-n)+1)n_{i}}{l_{3}}H_{1}\frac{\partial\tilde{g}_{1}}{\partial y}=\tilde{c}_{1}H^{n_{i}-1}\tilde{g}_{1}^{d_{1}(M-n)+1},\quad\tilde{c}_{1}\in K.

b)
\tilde{g}
has one root.

Then
\tilde{g}=(b+\tilde{\alpha}y)^{d}
for some
b\in K
. In this case the solution of the equation (
4.14
) is of the form
H=c(b+\tilde{\alpha}y)^{d(M-n)+1}
, hence
en_{1,0}(Q_{i})=(l(M-n)+1,d(M-n)+1)
. According to lemma
4.4
we have two possibilities:

ba)
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i})
is divisible by
d_{2}
, and therefore
st_{0,1}(Q_{i})
is proportional with
st_{0,1}(P)=(ln,dn)
(see the proof of item 6 in lemma). Let, say,
st_{0,1}(Q_{i})=(lA,dA)
. Then we have

l(M-n)+1\geq lA
and
dA\geq d(M-n)+1
, whence

l(M-n)+1\geq lA\geq l(M-n)+l/d.

Since
l<d
, we must have
lA=l(M-n)+1
, hence
dA=d(M-n)+d/l
. Again it is possible only if
d/l=d_{2}
. But then the vertex
st_{0,1}(Q_{i})
belongs to the
(1,0)
-top line of
Q_{i}
, and lies above the vertex
en_{1,0}(Q_{i})
– a contradiction. So, this case is impossible.

bb)
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i})
is not divisible by
d_{2}
. Then
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i})=d(M-n)+1
, and
st_{0,1}(Q_{i})=(l(M-n)+1,d(M-n)+1)
(see item 3.4. 7) of lemma). So,
st_{0,1}(Q_{i})=en_{1,0}(Q_{i})
and therefore
Q_{i}
is of subrectangular type. Besides, in this case
I=i
.

∎

5
DC-pairs and the string equation

In this section we study solutions to the
string equation

[Q,P]=1
, where
Q,P\in D_{1}
(the DC-pairs, or more generally, arbitrary endomorphisms of the first Weyl algebra, provide partial solutions to this equation). The natural question is how to classify, if possible, such solutions, and how they are controlled by their normal forms. We’ll show that solutions of this equation are connected with pairs of commuting ordinary differential operators generating a ring of rank one, and vice versa, any pair of commuting differential operators generating a ring of rank one, whose spectral curve is of some special type, provide a solution to the string equation. In the last section we’ll apply these results to show that there are no DC-pairs, i.e. the Dixmier conjecture is true. In this section we assume
K={\mbox{\dbl C}}
(
K
may be assumed to be any other algebraically closed field of characteristic zero).

Theorem
4.1
motivates the following definition.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
P,Q\in D_{1}
be two differential operators such that
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(P)=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(P)=p
,
\sigma(P)=\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(P)}
,
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q)=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(Q)=q
, and
[Q,P]=1
or
[Q,P]=0
. Assume that there exists a sequence of operators
Q_{i}:=F_{i}(P,Q)
,
i=0,\ldots,I
like in theorem
4.1
(i.e. defined by recursion in the same way) such that
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(Q_{i})
for all
i
. In this case we’ll define a sequence of numbers, called
sequential GCD
, as
23
23

23

We don’t assume here that
SeqGCD_{I}(P,Q)=1
.

SeqGCD_{i}(P,Q):=GCD(\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(P),\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{0}),\ldots,\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i})),\quad i=0,\ldots,I

and a
distance
of
P,Q
by the formula (
4.1
):
