Dist(P,Q):=\sum_{i=0}^{I-1}(n_{i}\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits F_{i}(P,Q)-\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits F_{i+1}(P,Q)).

The number
SeqGCD_{I}(P,Q)
will be called as the
end of sequential GCD sequence
if
SeqGCD_{I}(P,Q)<SeqGCD_{I-1}(P,Q)
.

The condition on orders imply that
\sigma(Q_{i})=c_{i}\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(Q_{i})}
,
c_{i}\in K
(see remark
2.2
). We’ll call the pairs of constants
(\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(Q_{0}),c_{0}),\ldots,(\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(Q_{I}),c_{I})
as the
sequence
of
P,Q
.

We formulated definition above in a slightly more general way than it was mentioned in Introduction. In most cases we’ll use it to describe the head-chopping process; in this case we’ll usually assume that
SeqGCD_{I}(P,Q)=1
and
SeqGCD_{I-1}(P,Q)>1
as in theorem
4.1
. As the sequences
F_{i}
are not uniquely determined, it is natural to ask if the distance depends on the choice of a sequence with the same end of sequential GCD sequence. It is not difficult to see that distance does not depend on such a choice, namely, if
F_{i}^{\prime}
,
i=0,\ldots,I^{\prime}
is another sequence of polynomials such that
SeqGCD_{I^{\prime}}(P,Q)=SeqGCD_{I}(P,Q)
and
SeqGCD_{I^{\prime}}(P,Q)<SeqGCD_{I^{\prime}-1}(P,Q)
,
SeqGCD_{I}(P,Q)<SeqGCD_{I-1}(P,Q)
, then both distances coincide. In particular, this follows from lemma below (an improved analogue of lemma
2.10
):

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\tilde{P}=\partial^{p},\tilde{Q}\in\hat{D}_{1}^{sym}
be two operators such that
\sigma(\tilde{Q})=c\partial^{q}
,
c\in K
, and
[\tilde{Q},\tilde{P}]=1
or
[\tilde{Q},\tilde{P}]=0
. Assume that there exists a sequence of operators
\tilde{Q}_{i}:=F_{i}(\tilde{P},\tilde{Q})
,
i=0,\ldots,I
like in theorem
4.1
(i.e. defined by recursion in the same way) such that
\sigma(\tilde{Q}_{i})=\tilde{c}_{i}\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}\tilde{Q}_{i}}
,
\tilde{c}_{i}\in K^{*}
for all
i
. Let

\tilde{d}:=SeqGCD_{I}(\tilde{P},\tilde{Q})=GCD(p,\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{Q}_{0}),\ldots,\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{Q}_{I})).

Then

1.

there exists a monic invertible operator
\tilde{S}\in K^{\oplus p}((\tilde{D}^{-1}))\subset E_{p}
with
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{\tilde{D}}(\tilde{S})=0
such that all coefficients of the operator
\tilde{S}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})\tilde{S}

satisfy the following condition (we’ll call such operator as auxiliary operator):

\tilde{S}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})\tilde{S}=c_{q}\tilde{D}^{q}+c_{q-k_{1}}\tilde{D}^{q-k_{1}}+\ldots+c_{q-k_{1}-\ldots-k_{I}}\tilde{D}^{q-k_{1}-\ldots-k_{I}}+\sum_{i<q-k_{1}-\ldots-k_{I}}q_{i}\tilde{D}^{i},

where
\quad c_{i}\in K
24
24

24

Recall that
K
is embedded to
K^{\oplus p}
as
a\mapsto(a,\ldots,a)
,
\quad q_{i}\in K^{\oplus p}
for
i\neq-p
(and for
i=-p

q_{i}
has a shape
c\Gamma+\tilde{q}_{i}
,
c\in K
,
\tilde{q}_{i}\in K^{\oplus p}
),
\quad[q_{i},\tilde{D}^{\tilde{d}}]=0
(and
\quad[\tilde{q}_{i},\tilde{D}^{\tilde{d}}]=0
)
25
25

25

We’ll call these coefficients, allowing for some freedom of speech,
\tilde{D}^{\tilde{d}}
-invariant
,
k_{i}=n_{i-1}\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{Q}_{i-1})-\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{Q}_{i})
.

Moreover, there exists such an operator
\tilde{S}
with additional conditions on its coefficients
s_{l}
:
s_{l,i}=0
for
i=0,\ldots,\tilde{d}-1
for all
l
.

In particular, if
\tilde{d}=1
, then all coefficients
q_{i}
are constant (i.e.
q_{i}
or
\tilde{q}_{i}
belong to
K
, in the same sense as above), so that
\tilde{S}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})\tilde{S}\in K((\tilde{D}^{-1}))
if
[\tilde{P},\tilde{Q}]=0
and
\tilde{S}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})\tilde{S}\in K[x]((\tilde{D}^{-1}))\subset K[\Gamma]((\tilde{D}^{-1}))
if
[\tilde{P},\tilde{Q}]=1
26
26

26

The ring of classical Schur’s pseudo-differential operators
E=K[[x]]((\tilde{D}^{-1}))
is naturally embedded to
E_{p}
by the following receipt: any series
P
from
E
can be represented as a sum of homogeneous components (HCPs) with respect to the
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}
function. Rewriting them in the G-form, we get a series from
E_{p}
. Besides, the homogeneous components of this series don’t depend on
A_{p,i}
and
B_{j}
and
Sdeg_{A}(P_{\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(P)-i})\leq i
for all
i>0
(if
P
is monic, then even
Sdeg_{A}(P_{\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(P)-i})<i
and
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(P)=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(P)
).
It’s easy to see that the image of the subring
D_{1}\subset E
under this embedding is characterised by the properties of theorem
2.3
, where we adopt definition
2.9
to the ring
E_{p}
in the following sense: condition 2 from definition (totally freeness) is replaced by an equivalent system of equations (
2.3
) on coefficients, all other conditions are the same. In particular, since the homomorphism
\hat{\Phi}
is an embedding, this image of
D_{1}
coincides with the image
\hat{\Phi}(D_{1})
of the subring
D_{1}\subset\hat{D}_{1}^{sym}

.

The constants
c_{i}
don’t depend on the choice of a sequence
F_{i}
with the same end of sequential GCD sequence.

2.

in case
[\tilde{Q},\tilde{P}]=1
we have

Dist(\tilde{P},\tilde{Q}):=\sum_{i=0}^{I-1}(n_{i}\mathop{\rm\bf ord}F_{i}(\tilde{P},\tilde{Q})-\mathop{\rm\bf ord}F_{i+1}(\tilde{P},\tilde{Q}))=k_{1}+\ldots+k_{I}<p+q,

and if
SeqGCD_{I-1}(\tilde{P},\tilde{Q})>\tilde{d}
, then
c_{q-k_{1}-\ldots-k_{I}}=c\tilde{c}_{I}\neq 0
.

In particular, the distance does not depend on the choice of a sequence
F_{i}
with the same end of sequential GCD sequence.

Remark 5.1
.

This lemma can be applied e.g. to a normal form
\tilde{Q}
of a differential operator
Q
with respect to
P\in D_{1}
, satisfying similar conditions. Namely, let
P,Q\in D_{1}
be two differential operators such that
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(P)=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(P)=p
,
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q)=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(Q)=q
, and
[Q,P]=1
or
[Q,P]=0
. Assume that there exists a sequence of operators
Q_{i}:=F_{i}(P,Q)
,
i=0,\ldots,I
like in theorem
4.1
(i.e. defined by recursion in the same way) such that
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(Q_{i})
for all
i
. Then the normal form of
Q
with respect to
P
satisfies conditions of lemma.

For, by remark
2.2
all operators
P,Q,Q_{i}
have invertible highest coefficients, hence they are regular. Therefore by proposition
2.2
and by basic properties of the order function from remark
2.1
normal forms of operators
Q_{i}
with respect to
P
have the same orders as
Q_{i}
, and their highest symbols have shape
c\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(Q_{i})}
,
c\in K
27
27

27

Clearly, without loss of generality
P
can be assumed to be normalized
.

For a pair of operators
\tilde{P},\tilde{Q}
from lemma the same notions as in definition
5.1
(i.e. sequential GCD, distance and sequence) are defined, and we’ll use them below.

Proof.

1. By proposition
2.1
and lemma
4.1
all homogeneous components of
\tilde{Q}
except maybe one (if
[\tilde{Q},\tilde{P}]=1
) belong to
C(\partial^{p})
, and if
[\tilde{Q},\tilde{P}]=1
, then the only one homogeneous component not from the centralizer is
\tilde{Q}_{-p}
of
Sdeg_{A}(\tilde{Q}_{-p})=1
and it has a shape
-(x\partial)/p+a
, where
a\in C(\partial^{p})
(its explicit shape, up to a constant, is determined in lemma
4.1
). Then all coefficients of the series
\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})
except maybe one belong to
K^{\oplus p}
, and if
[\tilde{Q},\tilde{P}]=1
, then the only one coefficient not from the centralizer has a shape
-(x\partial)/p+a
, where
a\in K^{\oplus p}
.

To prove our claim we apply lemma
2.10
to the operator
\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})
several (
I
) times. First, by this lemma and remark

there exists an invertible monic operator
\tilde{S}_{0}
from

such that all coefficients of the operator

commute with
\tilde{D}^{d_{1}}
(in the same sense as it is formulated), where

. For, it is claimed there in the case if
[\tilde{Q},\tilde{P}]=0
, and in the case
[\tilde{Q},\tilde{P}]=1
the proof is the same as in loc. cit. (and we’ll repeat it below for generic step), because by relations from lemma
2.2
and analogous relations in the ring
E_{p}
we have for any such
\tilde{S}\in K^{\oplus p}((\tilde{D}^{-1}))

\tilde{S}^{-1}\Gamma\tilde{D}^{-p}\tilde{S}=\Gamma\tilde{D}^{-p}+T,\quad T\in K^{\oplus p}((\tilde{D}^{-1})),\quad\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(T)<-p

(5.1)

and therefore all coefficients can be made, step by step,
\tilde{D}^{d_{1}}
-invariant. Then we claim that

\tilde{S}_{0}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})\tilde{S}_{0}=c_{q}\tilde{D}^{q}+c_{q-k_{1}}\tilde{D}^{q-k_{1}}+\mbox{terms of lower order},\quad c_{i}\in K,

where

. Indeed,

\tilde{Q}_{1}=\tilde{Q}^{n_{0}}-\varepsilon_{0}\partial^{pm_{0}}
, then
\tilde{S}_{0}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q}_{1})\tilde{S}_{0}=(\tilde{S}_{0}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})\tilde{S}_{0})^{n_{0}}-\varepsilon_{0}^{n_{0}}\tilde{D}^{pm_{0}}
has the highest symbol of the shape
c_{qn_{0}-k_{1}}\tilde{D}^{qn_{0}-k_{1}}
,
c_{qn_{0}-k_{1}}=\tilde{c}_{1}\in K^{*}
, whence

(\tilde{S}_{0}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})\tilde{S}_{0})^{n_{0}}=\varepsilon_{0}^{n_{0}}\tilde{D}^{qn_{0}}+c_{qn_{0}-k_{1}}\tilde{D}^{qn_{0}-k_{1}}+\mbox{terms of lower order},\quad c_{i}\in K.

Since all coefficients of
\tilde{S}_{0}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})\tilde{S}_{0}
are
\tilde{D}^{d_{1}}
-invariant, it must have the shape as claimed above, i.e.
c_{q-k_{1}}=\tilde{c}_{1}/(\varepsilon_{0}^{n_{0}}n_{0})\in K
.

Now we can find a monic invertible operator
\tilde{S}_{1}\in K^{\oplus p}((\tilde{D}^{-1}))\subset E_{p}
whose coefficients are
\tilde{D}^{d_{1}}
-invariant
and such that all coefficients of the operator
\tilde{S}_{1}^{-1}\tilde{S}_{0}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})\tilde{S}_{0}\tilde{S}_{1}
are
\tilde{D}^{d_{2}}
-invariant, where
d_{2}=SeqGCD_{1}(\tilde{P},\tilde{Q})
. The proof is the same as in lemma
2.10
(in both cases,
[\tilde{Q},\tilde{P}]=0
or
[\tilde{Q},\tilde{P}]=1
):

We will find the operator
\tilde{S}_{1}
as the limit of a Cauchy sequence
28
28

28

with respect to the topology defined by the pseudo-valuation
-\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{\tilde{D}}
. Assume

\tilde{S}_{0}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})\tilde{S}_{0}=c_{q}\tilde{D}^{q}+c_{q-k_{1}}\tilde{D}^{q-k_{1}}+\sum_{i<q-k_{1}}q_{i}\tilde{D}^{i},

where
\quad q_{i}\in K^{\oplus p}
for
i\neq-p
(and for
i=-p

q_{i}
has the shape
c\Gamma+\tilde{q}_{i}
,
\tilde{q}_{i}\in K^{\oplus p}
), and all coefficients are

-invariant. Let
q_{l}=(q_{l,0},\ldots,q_{l,p-1})
be the first coefficient not commuting with
\tilde{D}^{d_{2}}
29
29

29

Here we again consider the coefficients in the sense of formulation above, namely, if
Sdeg_{A}(q_{l})=1
, then the shape of

is
c\Gamma+\tilde{q}_{l}
,
\tilde{q}_{l}\in K^{\oplus p}
, and we consider
\tilde{q}_{l}
instead of
q_{l}
. The term

5.1
) given above.
. Consider the operator
\tilde{\tilde{S}}_{l}:=1+s_{l}\tilde{D}^{-l}
, where

and

30
30

30

The last condition just means that
s_{l,i}=s_{l,i+d_{1}\mbox{mod $p$}}
for all
i
.
. Then an easy direct calculation shows that

\tilde{\tilde{S}}_{l}^{-1}\tilde{S}_{0}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})\tilde{S}_{0}\tilde{\tilde{S}}_{l}=c_{q}\tilde{D}^{q}+c_{q-k_{1}}\tilde{D}^{q-k_{1}}+\ldots+(q_{q-k_{1}-l}+c_{q-k_{1}}(-s_{l}+\sigma^{q-k_{1}}(s_{l})))\tilde{D}^{q-k_{1}-l}+\\
\mbox{terms of lower order},

where
\ldots
are the same terms of
\tilde{S}_{0}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})\tilde{S}_{0}
(as

is
\tilde{D}^{d_{1}}
-invariant). Consider the following
d_{2}
systems of linear equations: for
i=0,\ldots,d_{2}-1
[mod
d_{2}
] set
b_{i}:=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\tilde{q}_{l,[(i+(q-k_{1})k)\mbox{mod $d_{1}$}]}
, where

is a minimal natural number such that
(q-k_{1})m=0\mbox{ mod $d_{1}$}

i
-th system is (below all indices are considered modulo

)

\begin{cases}\tilde{q}_{l,i}-s_{l,i}+s_{l,i+(q-k_{1})}=b_{i}/m\\
\tilde{q}_{l,i+(q-k_{1})}-s_{l,i+(q-k_{1})}+s_{l,i+2(q-k_{1})}=b_{i}/m\\
\ldots\\
\tilde{q}_{l,i+(m-1)(q-k_{1})}-s_{l,i+(m-1)(q-k_{1})}+s_{l,i}=b_{i}/m\end{cases}

(5.2)

This system is solvable and give explicit formulae for unknown variables
s_{l,j}
: for
r=1,\ldots,(m-1)
from the first
(m-1)
equations we get

s_{l,i+r(q-k_{1})}=rb_{i}/m+s_{l,i}-(\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}\tilde{q}_{l,i+j(q-k_{1})})

(5.3)

(
s_{l,i}
is a free parameter), and the last equation becomes an identity under substitution of these values. Taking such
s_{l}
we get

\tilde{\tilde{q}}_{l}:=\tilde{q}_{l}-s_{l}+\sigma^{(q-k_{1})}(s_{l})=(b_{0}/m,\ldots,b_{d_{2}-1}/m,b_{0}/m,\ldots,b_{d_{2}-1}/m,\ldots)

which obviously commutes with
\tilde{D}^{d_{2}}
. Note that
s_{l}
is uniquely determined if we put the free parameters

for
i=0,\ldots,d_{2}-1
.

It’s easy to see that the system
\{\prod_{i=1}^{j}\tilde{\tilde{S}}_{i}\}
,

is a Cauchy system in
E_{p}
, and its limit
\tilde{S}_{1}
is the needed operator. Note that all coefficients

for

must be constant, because

\tilde{S}_{1}^{-1}\tilde{S}_{0}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q}_{2})\tilde{S}_{0}\tilde{S}_{1}=\tilde{S}_{1}^{-1}\tilde{S}_{0}^{-1}(((\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q}))^{n_{0}}-\varepsilon_{0}^{n_{0}}\tilde{D}^{pm_{0}})^{n_{1}})\tilde{S}_{0}\tilde{S}_{1}-\varepsilon_{1}^{n_{1}}\tilde{D}^{pm_{1}}

has the highest symbol of the shape
\tilde{c}_{2}\tilde{D}^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}\tilde{Q}_{2}}
(if there was a non-constant coefficient

with
i\leq q-k_{1}-k_{2}
, this would be impossible).

Continuing this line of reasoning, after
I
steps we’ll find
I
operators
\tilde{S}_{0},\tilde{S}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{S}_{I-1}
such that the coefficients of the operator
\tilde{S}_{I-1}^{-1}\ldots\tilde{S}_{0}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})\tilde{S}_{0}\ldots\tilde{S}_{I-1}
are
\tilde{D}^{\tilde{d}}
-invariant, and

\tilde{S}_{I-1}^{-1}\ldots\tilde{S}_{0}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})\tilde{S}_{0}\ldots\tilde{S}_{I-1}=c_{q}\tilde{D}^{q}+c_{q-k_{1}}\tilde{D}^{q-k_{1}}+\ldots+c_{q-k_{1}-\ldots-k_{I}}\tilde{D}^{q-k_{1}-\ldots-k_{I}}+\sum_{i<q-k_{1}-\ldots-k_{I}}q_{i}\tilde{D}^{i},

(5.4)

where
\quad c_{i}\in K
,
\quad q_{i}\in K^{\oplus p}
for
i\neq-p
(and for
i=-p

q_{i}
has a shape
c\Gamma+\tilde{q}_{i}
),
\quad[q_{i},\tilde{D}^{\tilde{d}}]=0
(and
\quad[\tilde{q}_{i},\tilde{D}^{\tilde{d}}]=0
), and
k_{i}=n_{i-1}\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{Q}_{i-1})-\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{Q}_{i})
.

Moreover, we can find these operators
\tilde{S}_{k}
such that their coefficients satisfy additional conditions
s_{l,i}=0
for
i=0,\ldots,d_{k}-1
and all
l
, so the product
\tilde{S}:=\tilde{S}_{0}\ldots\tilde{S}_{I-1}
will satisfy the claimed condition on its coefficients from lemma.

Remark 5.2
.

The relation between coefficients
\tilde{c}_{i}
of the sequence
(\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{Q}_{i}),\tilde{c}_{i})
and coefficients
c_{i}
of the series
\tilde{S}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})\tilde{S}
is non-trivial, but let’s point out some observations.

First, obviously, the coefficients
\tilde{c}_{i}
can be uniquely reconstructed by the coefficients
c_{i}
if we know the exponents
n_{i}
of the sequence
F_{i}
. Vice versa, the coefficients
c_{i}
can be uniquely reconstructed by the coefficients
\tilde{c}_{i}
if we know the sequence
F_{i}
by solving recurrent system of equations (by comparing the corresponding highest coefficients of expressions
F_{i}(\tilde{D}^{p},\tilde{S}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})\tilde{S})
with
\tilde{c}_{i}
).

Second, by solving the system of equations on
c_{i}
, non-zero values of
c_{i}
with
i\leq q-k_{1}-\ldots-k_{l}
may appear only at exponents belonging to the lattice
\Lambda\subset{\mbox{\dbl Z}}
generated by
p
and
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{Q}_{j})
with
j\leq l
.

Third, the last observation shows that if
l
is such that
SeqGCD_{l}(\tilde{P},\tilde{Q})<SeqGCD_{l-1}(\tilde{P},\tilde{Q})
, then

GCD\{p,q,q-k_{1},\ldots,q-k_{1}-\ldots-k_{l}\}<GCD\{p,q,q-k_{1},\ldots,q-k_{1}-\ldots-k_{l-1}\},

q-k_{1}-\ldots-k_{l}
is the first exponent of the series from lemma with such a property, and

c_{q-k_{1}-\ldots-k_{l}}=\tilde{c}_{q-k_{1}-\ldots-k_{l}}/(n_{0}\cdots n_{l-1}\varepsilon_{0}^{n_{0}-1}\cdots\varepsilon_{l-1}^{n_{l-1}-1}).

(5.5)

By construction and remark
5.2
we see that coefficients
c_{i}
don’t depend on the choice of a sequence
F_{i}
with the same end of sequential GCD sequence.

2. To prove the last assertion of lemma let’s note that the distance is, by definition, a sum of distances between old and new head during the head-chopping process. In case of
[\tilde{Q},\tilde{P}]=1
this process is restricted by the distance between the head of
\tilde{Q}
and its tail
\tilde{Q}_{-p}
, because the highest symbol of the tail can not be of the shape
\tilde{c}\partial^{k}
.

The last assertion is therefore obvious in view of remark
5.2
.

∎

Now we are ready to prove the first theorem relating a solution to the string equation with a pair of commuting ordinary differential operators generating a ring of rank one. We will need a partial analogue of definition
5.1

Definition 5.2
.

Let
f(X,Y)\in K[X,Y]
be an irreducible polynomial of Burchnall-Chaundy type (cf. remark
2.9
),

f(X,Y)=(\alpha X^{n}+Y^{m})^{d}+\ldots,

where
\ldots
mean terms of lower weighted degree,
0\neq\alpha\in K
,
(m,n)=1
,
d\geq 1
. Let
Y=z^{-nd}
,
X=c_{-md}z^{-md}+c_{n_{1}}z^{n_{1}}+c_{n_{2}}z^{n_{2}}+\ldots\in K((z))
be a solution (a Puiseux series) of equation
f(X,Y)=0
31
31

31

Thus, by classification theorem
2.4
, there exist a pair of commuting differential operators
P,Q\in D_{1}
, such that
\mathop{\rm rk}K[P,Q]=1
and
K[P,Q]\simeq K[X,Y]/(f)
. By Wilson’s theorem (see remark
2.9
) their BC-polynomial
\partial Res(P-X,Q-Y)
is irreducible, therefore is proportional to
f
, whence

\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(P)=p=nd
,
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q)=q=md
.
Then, if
S_{cl}
is a classical Schur operator such that
S_{cl}^{-1}PS_{cl}=\partial^{nd}
, the operator
S_{cl}^{-1}QS_{cl}
has constant coefficients and has the shape of
X
(after formal replacement
\partial\mapsto z^{-1}
). Here
z
is a local parameter at the point ”at infinity” of the one-point compactification of the curve
f(X,Y)=0
.
. Assume
n_{l}
is the first exponent such that
GCD(d,n_{1},\ldots,n_{l})=1
.

Then we’ll call the number
nd-n_{l}
as
the distance of
f(X,Y)
.
For a solution
Y,X
other notions from definition
5.1
are defined (i.e. the sequential GCD and the sequence) in the same way.

Remark 5.3
.

Note that for fixed
Y=z^{-nd}
all other solutions of this equation can be obtained by the scale transform
z\mapsto\xi z
, where
\xi
is a
nd
-th primitive root of unity. Hence
c_{nd}=0
. Indeed, the sum of roots of the polynomial
f(X,z^{-nd})
,
\sum_{i=1}^{nd}X|_{z\mapsto z\xi^{i}}
, must be a polynomial in
z^{-1}
, hence
ndc_{nd}=0
.

Theorem 5.1

(cutting the tail)
.

Let
P,Q\in D_{1}
be two differential operators such that
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(P)=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(P)=p=nd
,
\sigma(P)=\partial^{\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(P)}
,
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q)=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(Q)=q=md
, where
(m,n)=1
, and
[Q,P]=1
. Assume that there exists a sequence of operators
Q_{i}:=F_{i}(P,Q)
,
i=0,\ldots,I
like in theorem
4.1
(i.e. defined by recursion in the same way) such that
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(Q_{i})
for all
i
. Assume
SeqGCD_{I}(P,Q)=1
and
SeqGCD_{I-1}(P,Q)>1
.
Let
\tilde{Q}=S^{-1}QS
be a normal form of
Q
with respect to
P
, where
S
is a monic Schur operator (see remark
2.3
).

Then the pair
(\partial^{p},\tilde{Q}-\tilde{Q}_{-p})
is a normal form of a commuting pair of formally elliptic differential operators
P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}
, where
P^{\prime}
is monic and normalized,
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(P^{\prime})=p
,
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q^{\prime})=q
, such that
rk(K[P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}])=1
(i.e.
\tilde{Q}-\tilde{Q}_{-p}
is a normal form of
Q^{\prime}
with respect to
P^{\prime}
).

Moreover, this pair satisfies conditions from lemma
5.1
, it has the same sequence as
(P,Q)
and its affine spectral curve is given by a BC-polynomial from definition
5.2
of distance
\leq p+q-1
.

Proof.

By lemma
5.1
there exists a monic invertible operator
\tilde{S}\in K^{\oplus p}((\tilde{D}^{-1}))\subset E_{p}
with
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{\tilde{D}}(\tilde{S})=0
such that

\tilde{S}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})\tilde{S}=c_{q}\tilde{D}^{q}+c_{q-k_{1}}\tilde{D}^{q-k_{1}}+\ldots+c_{q-k_{1}-\ldots-k_{I}}\tilde{D}^{q-k_{1}-\ldots-k_{I}}+\sum_{i<q-k_{1}-\ldots-k_{I}}q_{i}\tilde{D}^{i}\in K[x]((\tilde{D}^{-1})),

and
GCD(p,q,q-k_{1},\ldots,q-k_{1}-\ldots-k_{I})=1
.
Since
Dist(P,Q)<p+q
, we have

\tilde{S}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})\tilde{S}=\tilde{S}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q}-\tilde{Q}_{-p})\tilde{S}+\tilde{S}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q}_{-p})\tilde{S}=\\
c_{q}\tilde{D}^{q}+c_{q-k_{1}}\tilde{D}^{q-k_{1}}+\ldots+c_{q-k_{1}-\ldots-k_{I}}\tilde{D}^{q-k_{1}-\ldots-k_{I}}+\sum_{i<q-k_{1}-\ldots-k_{I}}\tilde{q}_{i}\tilde{D}^{i}+\tilde{S}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q}_{-p})\tilde{S},

where
\tilde{q}_{i}\in K^{\oplus p}
. Therefore we can find, following the proof of lemma
5.1
, another monic operator
\tilde{S}^{\prime}\in K^{\oplus p}((\tilde{D}^{-1}))\subset E_{p}
with
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits_{\tilde{D}}(\tilde{S}^{\prime})=0
such that

(\tilde{S}^{\prime})^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q}-\tilde{Q}_{-p})\tilde{S}^{\prime}=c_{q}\tilde{D}^{q}+c_{q-k_{1}}\tilde{D}^{q-k_{1}}+\ldots+c_{q-k_{1}-\ldots-k_{I}}\tilde{D}^{q-k_{1}-\ldots-k_{I}}+\ldots\in K((\tilde{D}^{-1})).

(5.6)

Since both operators
\tilde{S},\tilde{S}^{\prime}
are monic, the pair
(\partial^{p},\tilde{Q}-\tilde{Q}_{-p})
satisfies conditions from lemma
5.1
and has the same sequence as
(P,Q)
.

The rest of the proof is similar to
[
26
, Th.3.3]
. We define the space
W^{\prime}:=F^{\prime}\cdot(\tilde{S}\tilde{S}^{\prime})\subset K((\tilde{D}^{-1}))
, where
F^{\prime}=K[\tilde{D}]
. Set
B^{\prime}:=\hat{\Phi}(K[\partial^{p},\tilde{Q}-\tilde{Q}_{-p}])
, and
A^{\prime}:=(\tilde{S}^{\prime})^{-1}\tilde{S}^{-1}B^{\prime}\tilde{S}\tilde{S}^{\prime}\subset K((\tilde{D}^{-1}))
. Note that
(W^{\prime},A^{\prime})
is an embedded Schur pair of
rank one
32
32

32

Since
K[\partial^{p},\tilde{Q}-\tilde{Q}_{-p}]\in C(\partial^{p})\subset\hat{D}_{1}^{sym}
, we have
B^{\prime}\subset\hat{\Phi}(C(\partial^{p}))
and consists of Laurent polynomials whose coefficients at negative powers of
\tilde{D}
are not constants (see remark
2.6
). Since
\tilde{S}^{\prime},\tilde{S}
are monic and
A^{\prime}\subset K((\tilde{D}^{-1}))
, it implies that
A^{\prime}\cap K[[\tilde{D}^{-1}]]=K
. The rank is one because
SeqGCD_{I}(P,Q)=1
. The operators
\partial^{p}
,
\tilde{Q}-\tilde{Q}_{-p}
are algebraically dependent, and the polynomial
\det(\psi(\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q}-\tilde{Q}_{-p}))-X)\in K[X,\tilde{D}^{p}]
is a BC polynomial (after replacement
\tilde{D}^{p}\mapsto Y
(see
[
26
, Rem.3.3]
).
. Therefore, by theorem
2.5
it corresponds to a normalized ring
B\subset D_{1}
of commuting differential operators of rank one (see remark
2.9
),
B\simeq B^{\prime}
, so there are two formally elliptic commuting operators
P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}\in D_{1}
of the same orders
p,q
such that
B=K[P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}]
. By theorem
2.4
the ring
K[P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}]
determines the spectral curve
C
– the one-point compactification (with the help of the point
p
) of the affine spectral curve
C_{0}
determined by the above mentioned equation
f(X,Y)=0
(by Wilson’s theorem the polynomial
f
is irreducible, see remark
2.9
), and also a spectral sheaf
{{\cal F}}
- a torsion free sheaf of rank one. By
[
26
, Th. 3.3]
the corresponding (normalized) normal form of
B
is uniquely determined by this datum, and the correspondence is established via the embedded Schur pair of
B
. Since
B^{\prime}
and
B
have the same embedded Schur pairs, the operator

\tilde{Q}-\tilde{Q}_{-p}
is a normal form of
Q^{\prime}
with respect to
P^{\prime}
(see also remark below).

The last assertion follows immediately from formula (
5.6
).

∎

Remark 5.4
.

Note that the conditions of theorem
5.1
are satisfied for a DC pair
P,Q
of subrectangular pair from theorem
4.1
, and by this theorem
Dist(P,Q)=p+q-1
.

It can be shown that if
\varphi\in\mathop{\rm Aut}_{K}(A_{1})
, and
P=\varphi(x)
,
Q=\varphi(\partial)
, then these conditions hold as well. Moreover, in this case
Dist(P,Q)<p+q-1
, and the operators
P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}\in K[\partial]
, i.e. the corresponding spectral curve
C
from theorem
2.4
is rational.

However, it is an interesting question whether any solution of the string equation from
D_{1}
satisfy these conditions.

We’ll call the datum
(C,p,{{\cal F}},z)
as the
spectral datum
of a solution
P,Q
to the string equation. Here
z
is the local parameter at
p
mentioned above. The isomorphism class of this datum uniquely determines the equivalence class
[K[P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}]]
, cf.
[
26
, Rem. 3.5]
.

For any solution of the string equation
P,Q\in D_{1}
satisfying conditions of theorem
5.1
we can construct another solution
\bar{P},\bar{Q}\in D_{1}
such that
\bar{P}
is monic and normalized. We’ll call such pair a
normalized solution
. If the orders of operators
P,Q
are fixed, such a pair is uniquely determined in the conjugacy class by an invertible function from
K[[x]]^{*}
. Analogously, for any pair of commuting operators
P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}
as above we can construct a normalized pair (cf. remark
2.9
).

The subring
B^{\prime}=K[\partial^{p},\tilde{Q}^{\prime}]\subset\hat{D}_{1}^{sym}
generated by a normal form
\tilde{Q}^{\prime}
of
Q^{\prime}
with respect to
P^{\prime}
, can be also normalized in its conjugacy class (recall that a normal form is defined up to conjugation by an invertible element in
C(\partial^{p})
), e.g. by choosing a special set of generators as in definition
2.15
and applying lemma
2.11
. We’ll call the corresponding pair
(\partial^{p},\tilde{Q}^{\prime})
as a
normalized normal pair
. Analogously, since the normal form of the normalized solution
(P,Q)
is uniquely defined, by lemma
4.1
and theorem
5.1
, by the normal form of the corresponding normalized pair
(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
, we’ll call the normal form
(\partial^{p},\tilde{Q})
of a normalized solution
(P,Q)
as
normalized normal string pair
if the corresponding pair
(\partial^{p},\tilde{Q}-\tilde{Q}_{-p})
(from theorem
5.1
) is the normalized normal pair. More precisely:

Definition 5.3
.

A pair of elements
(\partial^{p},\tilde{Q}^{\prime})\in C(\partial^{p})\subset\hat{D}_{1}^{sym}
is called a
normal pair
of order
(p,q)
, where
q=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{Q}^{\prime})
, if it satisfies conditions of lemma
5.1
,
SeqGCD_{I}(\partial^{p},\tilde{Q}^{\prime})=1
,
SeqGCD_{I-1}(\partial^{p},\tilde{Q}^{\prime})>1
and
Dist(\partial^{p},\tilde{Q}^{\prime})<p+q
.
33
33

33

In this case the same arguments as in the proof of theorem
5.1
show that the polynomial
f(X,Y):=\det(\psi(\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q}^{\prime}))-X)|_{\tilde{D}^{p}\mapsto Y}\in K[X,Y]
is irreducible of distance
<p+q
(by Wilson’s theorem, because the corresponding ring of commuting differential operators has rank one).

This pair is called a
normalized normal pair
of order
(p,q)
if the ring
B^{\prime}:=K[\partial^{p},\tilde{Q}^{\prime}]
is a normalized normal form of rank one with respect to some set of generators (see definition
2.15
).

Definition 5.4
.

A pair of elements
(\partial^{p},\tilde{Q})\in\hat{D}_{1}^{sym}
is called a
normal string pair
of order
(p,q)
, where
q=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\tilde{Q})
, if
[\tilde{Q},\partial^{p}]=1
and the pair

(\partial^{p},\tilde{Q}-\tilde{Q}_{-p})
is a normal pair of order
(p,q)
.

This pair is called a
normalized normal string pair
of order
(p,q)
if the pair
(\partial^{p},\tilde{Q}-\tilde{Q}_{-p})
is a normalized normal pair of order
(p,q)
.

The normalisation depends on the choice of two specific generators in the ring
B^{\prime}
. Let’s fix such generators
\partial^{p}
,
F_{I}(\partial^{p},\tilde{Q}^{\prime})
, where
F_{i}
,
i=0,\ldots,I
is the sequence of polynomials like in theorem
5.1
(it exists by definition of distance), i.e.
F_{I}(\partial^{p},\tilde{Q}^{\prime})
is the first operator in this sequence of coprime order with
p
.

Let’s note that the normalisation can be made with the help of monic zeroth order invertible operator
S
from the centralizer
C(\partial^{p})
, according to the proof of lemma
2.11
in (
[
26
, Lem 3.4]
). So, this is an operator satisfying condition
A_{p}(0)
according to the description of
C(\partial^{p})
given in proposition
2.1
. Note also that any such operator commutes with the tail
\tilde{Q}_{-p}
. For,
[S^{-1}\tilde{Q}_{-p}S,\partial^{p}]=1
, and
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(S^{-1}\tilde{Q}_{-p}S)=-p
. So, by proposition
2.1
and lemma
4.1

S^{-1}\tilde{Q}_{-p}S=\tilde{Q}_{-p}
.

Theorem 5.2
.

There is a one-to-one correspondence between

1.

Normalized solutions
P,Q\in D_{1}
to the string equation such that
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(P)=p=nd
,
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q)=q=md
, where
(m,n)=1
, and conditions of theorem
5.1
are satisfied;

2.

Normalized normal string pairs
(\partial^{p},\tilde{Q})\in\hat{D}_{1}^{sym}
of order
p=nd,q=md
with respect to the fixed normalisation (e.g. as above);

with the same sequences. This correspondence is established with the help of conjugation by a unique monic invertible Schur operator
S\in\hat{D}_{1}^{sym}
of order zero satisfying condition
A_{p}(0)
.

Proof.

If
P,Q
is a normalized solution from item 1, then by proposition
2.4
there exists a monic Schur operator satisfying condition
A_{p}(0)
such that
S^{-1}PS=\partial^{p}
. Then,
clearly, the normal form
(\partial^{p},\tilde{Q})
with respect to
P
(and
S
) is a normal string pair with the same sequence by theorem
5.1
. Taking the normalisation, we get a normalized normal string pair. Since the normalisation can be made with the help of monic zeroth order invertible operator from the centralizer
C(\partial^{p})
, therefore, their product is a monic zeroth order invertible operator satisfying condition
A_{p}(0)
by lemma
2.8
. By lemma
2.11
the resulting Schur operator is uniquely defined.

Vice versa, if we have a normalized normal string pair
(\partial^{p},\tilde{Q})\in\hat{D}_{1}^{sym}
of order
p=nd,q=md
, we can construct a unique normalised solution of order
p,q
from item 1 as follows.

Step
1.
First, we claim that there is a unique invertible operator
\tilde{S}\in K^{\oplus p}((\tilde{D}^{-1}))
of order zero from lemma
5.1
such that
\tilde{S}^{-1}(\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q}))\tilde{S}\in K[x]((\tilde{D}^{-1}))
and for all
i
the coefficients
s_{i}
of
\tilde{S}
satisfy the condition
s_{i,0}=0
.

We know from lemma
5.1
that such an operator exists (as
SeqGCD_{I}(\partial^{p},\tilde{Q})=1
according to definition), so we need only to prove its uniqueness.

Assume there are two such operators,
\tilde{S}_{1},\tilde{S}_{2}
, and
\bar{Q}_{1}:=\tilde{S}_{1}^{-1}(\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q}))\tilde{S}_{1}
,
\bar{Q}_{2}:=\tilde{S}_{2}^{-1}(\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q}))\tilde{S}_{2}
,
\bar{Q}_{i}\in K[x]((\tilde{D}^{-1}))
. Then
\tilde{S}_{2}^{-1}\tilde{S}_{1}\bar{Q}_{1}\tilde{S}_{1}^{-1}\tilde{S}_{2}=\bar{Q}_{2}
. Then, if
F(X,Y)\in K[X,Y]
is any polynomial, we have also

\tilde{S}_{2}^{-1}\tilde{S}_{1}F(\tilde{D}^{p},\bar{Q}_{1})\tilde{S}_{1}^{-1}\tilde{S}_{2}=F(\tilde{D}^{p},\bar{Q}_{2}).

(5.7)

Note that
F(\tilde{D}^{p},\bar{Q}_{j})\in K[x]((\tilde{D}^{-1}))
and there exists a polynomial
F
such that the operator
F(\tilde{D}^{p},\bar{Q}_{j})
have order
M
coprime with
p
.

An easy direct calculation shows that the operators
\tilde{S}_{i}^{-1},\tilde{S}_{1}^{-1}\tilde{S}_{2}
satisfy the same property on coefficients (i.e. the
0
th components are zero). As in the proof of lemma
5.1
we can represent the operator
\tilde{S}_{1}^{-1}\tilde{S}_{2}
as a product of elementary operators
\tilde{S}_{l}
. Assume
l
is a minimal number such that
\tilde{S}_{l}
is non-trivial. Then the system of equations on
l
-th coefficients of operators in (
5.7
) will look like system
(
5.2
) with
i=0
,
k_{1}=0
,
m=p
where
q_{l}
is a constant, since the operators
\bar{Q}_{i}
have constant coefficients (i.e. all coefficients of polynomials in
x
belong to
K\subset K^{\oplus p}
), namely:

\begin{cases}q_{l}-s_{l,0}+s_{l,M}=b_{l}\\
q_{l}-s_{l,M}+s_{l,2M}=b_{l}\\
\ldots\\
q_{l}-s_{l,(p-1)M}+s_{l,0}=b_{l}\end{cases}

(5.8)

(here
q_{l}
and
b_{l}
are corresponding
constant
coefficients of
F(\tilde{D}^{p},\bar{Q}_{j})
). But, since
s_{l,0}=0
, this is possible only if all
s_{l,i}
are zero and
q_{l}=b_{l}
. Therefore,
\tilde{S}_{1}=\tilde{S}_{2}
.

Remark 5.5
.

Any monic invertible zeroth order operator
S\in K^{\oplus p}((\tilde{D}^{-1}))
from lemma
5.1
can be decomposed into a product
\tilde{S}\bar{S}
, where
\tilde{S}
is the operator as above, i.e. its coefficients satisfy the condition
s_{i,0}=0
, and
\bar{S}\in K((\tilde{D}^{-1}))
.

This is almost evident:
\bar{S}
can be constructed again as a product of elementary operators
\bar{S}_{l}=1+\bar{s}_{l}\tilde{D}^{-i}
,
\bar{s}_{l}\in K
, where each next coefficient
\bar{s}_{l}
is determined by the first non-zero component of coefficients of the operator
\tilde{S}\bar{S}_{1}\ldots\bar{S}_{l-1}
.

Next, let’s note that if we fix the operator
L:=\tilde{S}^{-1}(\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q}))\tilde{S}\in K[x]((\tilde{D}^{-1}))
in Step 1, there is a unique monic zeroth order operator
S\in K^{\oplus p}((\tilde{D}^{-1}))
such that
S^{-1}(\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q}))S=L
.

Indeed, we know that
S=\tilde{S}\bar{S}
, and
L
is a sum of the term
\Gamma\tilde{D}^{-p}/p=x\tilde{D}^{-p+1}/p
and a series with constant coefficients (which commutes with
\bar{S}
). From formula (
5.1
) it follows that
\bar{S}
commutes with the first term only if
\bar{S}=1
.

Step
2.
Let’s prove that there exists a unique monic operator
S\in E_{p}
of order zero satisfying condition
A_{p}(0)
such that
T:=\tilde{S}S
, where
\tilde{S}
is the unique operator constructed above in Step 1., is a monic zeroth order operator such that all its homogeneous components don’t depend on
A_{i}
and
B_{j}
and
Sdeg_{A}(T_{-i})<i
for all
i>0
34
34

34

(clearly, all components of
T
will be HCPs by lemma
2.5
, not depending on
B_{j}
since components of
\tilde{S},S
don’t depend on
B_{j}
). Here we adopt definition
2.9
to our situation in the following sense: condition 2 from definition (totally freeness) is replaced by an equivalent system of equations (
2.3
) on coefficients, all other conditions are the same. Since
\hat{\Phi}
is an injective homomorphism, any operator in
E_{p}
satisfying condition
A_{p}(0)
in this sense is an image of an operator satisfying condition
A_{p}(0)
, and vice versa
.

In view of corollary
2.1
this claim is equivalent to the following: there is a unique monic zeroth order operator
T
with all its homogeneous components don’t depending on
A_{i}
and
B_{j}
and
Sdeg_{A}(T_{-i})<i
for all
i>0
, such that
\tilde{S}T
is a monic zeroth order operator satisfying condition
A_{p}(0)
.

The proof is by induction on the index of coefficients of the operator
S=\tilde{S}T
. Clearly, it is monic and all its homogeneous components are HCP. For
s_{-1}
we have
s_{-1}=\tilde{s}_{-1}+t_{-1}
, whence
Sdeg_{A}(S_{-1})=0
iff
Sdeg_{A}(T_{-1})=0
. So,
s_{-1},t_{-1}\in K^{\oplus p}
and
s_{-1}
is totally free of
B_{j}
iff
s_{-1,0}=0
, because
s_{-1}=\Phi(\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}a_{i}A_{i})
and
s_{-1,0}=\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}a_{i}
and the system (
2.3
) reduces to just one equation

\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}a_{i}=0.

Analogously,
t_{-1}
is totally free of
B_{j}
iff
t_{-1,0}=0
. Since
\tilde{s}_{-1,0}=0
, we get
s_{-1}
is totally free of
B_{j}
iff
t_{-1}
is totally free of
B_{j}
. Since
t_{-1}
must not contain
A_{i}
, there is a unique value of
t_{-1}
with all these properties,
t_{-1}=0
.

Now suppose we have proved that there are uniquely determined coefficients
t_{-1},\ldots,t_{-l+1}
such that
s_{-1},\ldots,s_{-l+1}
satisfy condition
A_{p}(0)
. Now

s_{-l}=t_{-l}+\tilde{s}_{-l}+\sum_{i=1}^{l-1}\tilde{s}_{-i}\sigma^{i}(t_{-l+i}).

(5.9)

We need to show that there is a unique
t_{-l}
with
Sdeg_{A}(T_{-l})<l
and
t_{-l}
does not contain
A_{i}
and
B_{j}
such that
s_{-l}
is totally free of
B_{j}
and
Sdeg_{A}(S_{-l})<l
. So,
t_{-l}
should be of shape
t_{-l}=\sum_{m=0}^{l-1}a_{m,0}\Gamma_{m}
. Clearly,
Sdeg_{A}(s_{-l})<l
if
Sdeg_{A}(t_{-l})<l
, by induction hypothesis. The system (
2.3
) for the coefficients of
s_{-l}
has the shape

\sum_{m=0}^{l-1}(j-1)^{m}a_{m,0}=*

for
1\leq j\leq l
, where
*
means the known coefficients coming from the right hand side of (
5.9
). For
j=1
this system implies
a_{0,0}=0
, because
\tilde{s}_{-i,0}=0
for all
i
; so it reduces to analogous system

\sum_{m=1}^{l-1}(j-1)^{m}a_{m,0}=*

for
2\leq j\leq l
. Now observe that the matrix of this linear system has a shape of the Vandermonde matrix with powers of natural numbers, i.e. it is invertible, and there is a unique solution
a_{m,0}
for any values
*
. Hence
t_{l}
is uniquely determined.

Finally, note that
(\tilde{S}S)^{-1}=S^{-1}\tilde{S}^{-1}
is also a monic zeroth order operator such that all its homogeneous components don’t contain
A_{i}
(this easily follows from lemmas
2.4
and
2.6
), and
\tilde{S}^{-1}
is an operator with the same properties as
\tilde{S}
. So, analogous statement for the product
S\tilde{S}
holds.

Remark 5.6
.

If we take instead of
\tilde{S}
any operator
\tilde{S}\bar{S}
from Step 2., then the same arguments work, and there exists a unique monic zeroth order operator
S
satisfying condition
A_{p}(0)
such that
S\tilde{S}\bar{S}
satisfy properties of this step. Clearly, we can just take the same
S
as for
\tilde{S}
(as
\bar{S}
has constant coefficients). So, the claim of this step holds for
any
operator
\tilde{S}
from lemma
5.1
.

Note that
T
belongs to the image of the embedding
E\subset E_{p}
, i.e. it is a pseudo-differential operator; besides our calculations imply
1\cdot T=T(0)=1
, i.e.
T
is normalised. Since
F^{\prime}\cdot S-F^{\prime}
, we get from the Sato theorem
2.7
that
T=S_{cl}^{-1}
, where
S_{cl}
is the classical Sato operator for the space
W=F^{\prime}\cdot\tilde{S}
, i.e.
W=F^{\prime}\cdot S_{cl}
. It is not difficult to see that
S_{cl}
is also normalised.

Step
3. Put
\tilde{Q}^{\prime}:=\tilde{S}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})\tilde{S}
, where
\tilde{S}
is from Step 1. Let
S
be an operator from Step 4. such that all homogeneous components of
T:=S\tilde{S}
don’t contain
A_{i}
and
B_{j}
and
Sdeg_{A}(T_{-i})<i
for all
i>0
. As it was noticed in 4.,
S
is the image of some uniquely defined operator
S^{\prime}\in\hat{D}_{1}^{sym}
with the same properties under the map
\hat{\Phi}
.

We claim that
P:=S^{\prime}\partial^{p}(S^{\prime})^{-1},Q:=S^{\prime}(\tilde{Q})(S^{\prime})^{-1}\in D_{1}
is the normalised solution from item 1.

Indeed,
S\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})S^{-1}=(S\tilde{S})\tilde{Q}^{\prime}(\tilde{S}^{-1}S^{-1})=T\tilde{Q}^{\prime}T^{-1}
. Note that all homogeneous components of
T^{-1}
don’t contain
A_{i}
and
B_{j}
. Therefore, the product
T\tilde{Q}^{\prime}T^{-1}
satisfy the same property. Finally,
S\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})S^{-1}
satisfy condition
A_{p}(0)
, because
S,S^{-1},\tilde{Q}
satisfy it (see example
2.1
, lemma
2.8
, proposition
2.5
). Then by theorem
2.3

S\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})S^{-1}
is the image of the differential operator
Q
under the map
\hat{\Phi}
. It is monic, since
S
is monic. The same arguments work for
P
; moreover,
P
is normalized
35
35

35

So,
T
is a classical (normalised) Schur operator.
.

At last, note that the map from item 1 to item 2 and vice versa are inverse to each other. In one way because of uniqueness of the operator
S^{\prime}
, in another way because of lemma
2.11
.

∎

Remark 5.7
.

There is a natural question what can be said about the correspondence from theorem
5.2
if we allow conjugation by an arbitrary invertible operator.

By propositions
2.4
and
2.1
any such operator
S\in\hat{D}_{1}^{sym}
with the property
S^{-1}PS=\partial^{p}
is a product of a monic zeroth order operator satisfying condition
A_{p}(0)
and an invertible operator from the centralizer
C(\partial^{p})
. If we demand the normal form
(\partial^{p},\tilde{Q})
satisfies conditions of lemma
5.1
, then it can be shown by arguments from the proof of
[
26
, Lem. 3.4]
that
\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(S)=0
(the main reason is that the sequence
\tilde{Q}_{i}
contains an operator of the order coprime with
p
). Any invertible operator of order zero from
C(\partial^{p})
is a product of an invertible homogeneous operator of order 0, say
S_{0}
, and a monic zeroth order operator satisfying condition
A_{p}(0)
. Again, conditions of lemma
5.1
imply that
S_{0}
should be of special form. Namely, conjugation by
S_{0}
should be a scale transform
\partial\mapsto\xi_{nd}^{l}\partial
, where
\xi_{nd}
is a primitive
nd
-th root of unity. Of course, such a scale transform does not preserve the sequence of
P,Q
.

Remark 5.8
.

Analogous theorem (just with the same formulation) holds also for a normalized pair of commuting operators
(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
of orders
p,q
and satisfying conditions from remark
5.1
and theorem
5.1
, and normalized normal pairs of order
(p,q)
:

Theorem 5.3
.

There is a one-to-one correspondence between

1.

Normalized pairs
P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}\in D_{1}
of commuting operators such that
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(P)=p=nd
,
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q)=q=md
, where
(m,n)=1
, and conditions of theorem
5.1
and remark
5.1
are satisfied;

2.

Normalized normal pairs
(\partial^{p},\tilde{Q}^{\prime})\in\hat{D}_{1}^{sym}
of order
p=nd,q=md
with respect to the fixed normalisation;

with the same sequences. This correspondence is established with the help of conjugation by a unique monic invertible Schur operator
S\in\hat{D}_{1}^{sym}
of order zero satisfying condition
A_{p}(0)
.

This theorem can be deduced from the classification theory of commuting ordinary differential operators like in
[
26
, Th. 3.3]
or just by the same arguments as in the proof of theorem
5.1
.

In both cases (normalized string pairs or normalized commutative pairs) we can choose different normalisations. Directly from the proof we see that the corresponding normalized solutions of the string equation or normalized commuting operators are uniquely determined and in fact don’t depend on normalisation.

Vice versa, any pair of commuting ordinary differential operators of rank one with a specific spectral curve lead to a solution of the string equation:

Theorem 5.4

(glueing the tail)
.

Let
P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}\in D_{1}
,
[P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}]=0
be monic operators such that
\mathop{\rm rk}(K[P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}])=1
,
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(P^{\prime})=p=nd
,
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q^{\prime})=q=md
, where
(m,n)=1
. Let
f(X,Y)
be their irreducible BC-polynomial. Assume
f
is of distance
\leq p+q-1
. Let
\tilde{Q}^{\prime}=S^{-1}Q^{\prime}S
be a normal form of
Q^{\prime}
with respect to
P^{\prime}
, where
S
is a monic Schur operator (see remark
2.3
).

Then the pair
(\partial^{p},\tilde{Q^{\prime}}+\tilde{Q}_{-p})
, where
\tilde{Q}_{-p}
is a ”tail” defined in lemma
4.1
, is a normal form of some solution
(P,Q)\in D_{1}
to the string equation
[Q,P]=1
, where
P,Q
are monic and
P
is normalized (i.e.
\tilde{Q^{\prime}}+\tilde{Q}_{-p}
is a normal form of
Q
with respect to
P
). Moreover, this pair satisfies conditions from lemma
5.1
, it has the same sequence as
(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
.

In particular,
P,Q
satisfy conditions of theorem
5.1
.

Proof.

Since the distance of
f
is less than
p+q
, the operators
P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}
satisfy conditions of remark
5.1
with
SeqGCD_{I}(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})=1
and
Dist(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})<p+q
(see the footnote to definition
5.2
). Hence the operators
\partial^{p},\tilde{Q}^{\prime}
satisfy conditions of lemma
5.1
with the same sequential GCD and distance.

Now note that the operators
\partial^{p},\tilde{Q}^{\prime}+\tilde{Q}_{-p}
also satisfy these conditions and therefore by lemma
5.1
there exists a monic invertible operator
\tilde{S}
of order zero such that
\tilde{S}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q}^{\prime}+\tilde{Q}_{-p})\tilde{S}\in K[x]((\tilde{D}^{-1}))\subset E_{p}
.

The key observation is that the ring
K[\partial^{p},\tilde{Q}^{\prime}+\tilde{Q}_{-p}]
preserves
F=K[\partial]
(see section
2
for notation), i.e.
F\cdot K[\partial^{p},\tilde{Q}^{\prime}+\tilde{Q}_{-p}]\subseteq F
, because of special form of the ”tail”. Therefore the subring
A^{\prime}:=\tilde{S}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(K[\partial^{p},\tilde{Q}^{\prime}+\tilde{Q}_{-p}])\tilde{S}\subset E\subset E_{p}
preserves the space
W^{\prime}:=F^{\prime}\cdot\tilde{S}\subset E
. Note that
W^{\prime}
has support equal to
F^{\prime}
, because
\tilde{S}
is a monic invertible operator of order zero. Then by Sato’s theorem
2.7
there exists a classical Sato operator
S_{cl}\in E\subset E_{p}
such that
W^{\prime}=F^{\prime}\cdot S_{cl}
. Hence the subring
G:=S_{cl}A^{\prime}S_{cl}^{-1}
stabilises
F^{\prime}
, and therefore by another Sato theorem
2.6

G\subset D_{1}\subset E_{p}
. The subring
D_{1}\subset E_{p}
is characterised by the properties of theorem
2.3
, and therefore it coincides with the image of the subring
D_{1}\subset\hat{D}_{1}^{sym}
under the embedding
\hat{\Phi}
.
In particular,

S_{cl}\tilde{S}^{-1}\tilde{D}^{p}\tilde{S}S_{cl}^{-1}
and
S_{cl}\tilde{S}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q}^{\prime}+\tilde{Q}_{-p})\tilde{S}S_{cl}^{-1}
are the images of differential operators
P,Q
providing a solution to the string equation. Note that by construction
P
is a normalized operator, the orders of
P,Q
are
p,q
correspondingly.

The last assertion of theorem is clear. Besides, by theorem
5.2
we showed that the normalized pair of differential operators
P,Q
(i.e. with
P
normalised) with
[Q,P]=0
or
[Q,P]=1
is uniquely determined by its normalized (string) pair (in particular, the conjugating operator
\tilde{S}S_{cl}^{-1}
for which
P,Q
are differential operators, is uniquely determined). So, the operator
\tilde{Q^{\prime}}+\tilde{Q}_{-p}
is a normal form of
Q
with respect to
P
.
∎

Corollary 5.1
.

There is a one-to-one correspondence between

1.

Normalized solutions
P,Q\in D_{1}
of the string equation such that
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(P)=p=nd
,
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q)=q=md
, where
(m,n)=1
, and conditions of theorem
5.1
are satisfied;

2.

Normalized normal string pairs
(\partial^{p},\tilde{Q})\in\hat{D}_{1}^{sym}
of order
p=nd,q=md
with respect to the fixed normalisation as in theorem
5.2
;

3.

Normalized pair of commuting operators
P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}\in D_{1}
such that
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(P^{\prime})=p=nd
,
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q^{\prime})=q=md
, where
(m,n)=1
, and conditions of theorem
5.4
are satisfied;

4.

Normalized normal pairs
(\partial^{p},\tilde{Q}^{\prime})\in\hat{D}_{1}^{sym}
of order
p=nd,q=md
with respect to the fixed normalisation as in theorem
5.2
;

5.

Spectral datum
(C,p,{{\cal F}},z)
as in remark
5.4
, where
C
is the one point compactification of the affine spectral curve
C_{0}
determined by an equation
f(X,Y)=0
from definition
5.2
,
{{\cal F}}
is a torsion free sheaf of rank one on
C
,
z
is a formal local parameter at the point
p
;

with the same sequences. Here the sequence for the spectral datum is taken for some solution
Y=z^{-nd}
,
X=c_{-md}z^{-md}+\ldots
as in definition
5.2
.

The proof is clear in view of theorems
5.1
,
5.2
,
5.4
and remark
5.8
.

The last result related to the solutions of the string equation is a correspondence for adjoint differential operators - adjoint solutions of the string equation.

Recall that there is an anti-isomorphism of the ring
D_{1}
, denoted by
*:D_{1}\rightarrow D_{1}
,
*(x):=x
,
*(\partial):=-\partial
. If
P,Q
is a normalized solution to the string equation, then, clearly,
*(P),-*(Q)
is another normalized solution to the string equation. Now a natural question appears: how are the corresponding normalized pairs of commuting operators from corollary
5.1
related?

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
P,Q\in D_{1}
be a normalized solution of the string equation such that
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(P)=p=nd
,
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q)=q=md
, where
(m,n)=1
, and conditions of theorem
5.1
are satisfied. Let
P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}\in D_{1}
be the corresponding normalized pair of commuting operators from corollary
5.1
.
Let
*(P),-*(Q)\in D_{1}
be the adjoint normalized solution of the string equation.

Then the corresponding normalized pair of commuting operators from corollary
5.1
is
*(P^{\prime}),-*(Q^{\prime})
, in other words the diagram (
1.5
) holds.

Proof.

The anti-isomorphism
*
can not be extended to the whole ring
\hat{D}_{1}^{sym}
, but it can be extended to the ring
E_{p}
as follows:
*(A_{p,i}):=A_{p,p-i}
,
*(\tilde{D})=-\tilde{D}
,
*(\Gamma)=-\partial x=-\Gamma-1
. A direct check show that such extended
*
is indeed an anti-isomorphism of
E_{p}
.

Using this extension we can prove our claim in the ring
E_{p}
, since
\hat{\Phi}|_{D_{1}}
is the embedding of rings compatible with
*
.

Let
(\partial^{p},\tilde{Q})
be the corresponding to
P,Q
normalized normal string pair.
By theorem
5.2
we know that
\tilde{D}^{p}=S^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(P)S
,

\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})=S^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(Q)S
for a monic zeroth order operator
S\in E_{p}
satisfying condition
A_{p}(0)
. Then
*(\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q}))=*(S)*(\hat{\Phi}(Q))(*(S))^{-1}
and
*(\tilde{D}^{p})=*(S)*(\hat{\Phi}(P))(*(S))^{-1}
. Clearly, the pair
(*(\tilde{D}^{p}),-*(\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q})))
is a normal string pair of order
(p,q)
with the same sequence as the adjoint normalized solution
*(P),-*(Q)
(by remark
5.8
the operator
*(S)
is monic zeroth order and satisfy condition
A_{p}(0)
), and the pair
(*(\tilde{D}^{p}),-*(\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q}-\tilde{Q}_{-p})))
is a normal pair of order
(p,q)
with the same sequence as well.

According to the proof of theorem
5.2
and remark
5.8
there is an operator
\tilde{S}\in K^{\oplus p}((\tilde{D}^{-1}))
such that
\Omega:=\tilde{S}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q}-\tilde{Q}_{-p})\tilde{S}\in K((\tilde{D}^{-1}))
. Then also
*(\Omega)=*(\tilde{S})*(\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q}-\tilde{Q}_{-p}))*(\tilde{S}^{-1})\in K((\tilde{D}^{-1}))
.

From the proof of theorem
5.2
we know that the operator
T=S_{1}\tilde{S}\in E\subset E_{p}
(where
S_{1}
is the monic zeroth order operator from Step 2 of the proof of theorem
5.2
such that
\tilde{D}^{p}=S_{1}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(P^{\prime})S_{1}
,
\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q}-\tilde{Q}_{-p})=S_{1}^{-1}\hat{\Phi}(Q^{\prime})S_{1}
) is a classical Schur operator for the pair
P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}
, i.e.
\hat{\Phi}(P^{\prime})=T\tilde{D}^{p}T^{-1}
,
\hat{\Phi}(Q^{\prime})=T\Omega T^{-1}
.

Then
*(T)^{-1}=*(S_{1})^{-1}*(\tilde{S})^{-1}
is the classical Schur operator for the pair
*(P^{\prime}),-*(Q^{\prime})
36
36

36

i.e.
*(T)^{-1}*(\tilde{D}^{p})*(T)=\hat{\Phi}(*(P^{\prime}))
,
\hat{\Phi}(-*(Q^{\prime}))=-*(T)^{-1}*(\Omega)*(T)
.
, and the pair
(*(\tilde{D}^{p}),-*(\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q}-\tilde{Q}_{-p})))
is a normal pair of order
(p,q)
with the same sequence as
*(P^{\prime}),-*(Q^{\prime})
. Obviously, the pair
*(P^{\prime}),-*(Q^{\prime})
is a normalized pair of commuting operators. So, by corollary
5.1
the pair
*(P^{\prime}),-*(Q^{\prime})
is the corresponding normalized pair of commuting operators for
*(P),-*(Q)
.

∎

Remark 5.9
.

In
[
28
, §6]
it was shown that adjoint rings of commuting operators correspond to the
Serre dual
spectral datum, which in our case means that the pair
*(P^{\prime}),-*(Q^{\prime})
corresponds to the datum
(C,p,{{\cal F}}^{*}\otimes K_{C},z)
, where
K_{C}
is the canonical sheaf on
C
.

6
Proof of theorem
1.1

In this section we give the proof of theorem
1.1
. The scheme of the proof is as follows. Assuming that there is a DC pair
P,Q
of subrectangular type we construct first another DC-pair

P_{n},Q_{n}
of subrectangular type with additional beautiful properties: the right vertical sides of their rectangulars are monomials, and the upper horizontal sides have specific coefficients preceding the highest coefficients.

After that we construct another DC-pair
\Phi_{N,1}(P_{n}),\Phi_{N,1}(Q_{n})
by applying additional automorphism
\Phi_{N,1}
with appropriate
N
for this pair. Then we show (lemma
6.2
) that there are two cases, depending on the original DC-pair, such that in the first case the new DC pair has a sequence (of operators
Q_{N,i}
etc.) of length one more than the length of the sequence for the original DC pair, and the second case of the same length. In both cases we calculate sequential GCDs, distances and the orders of the last operators in the sequences. In both cases this DC-pair satisfies the termination condition for the head-chopping process. Lemma
6.2
relies heavily on the preparatory theorems
4.1
,
4.2
.

After that in section
6.2
we prove theorem
1.1
with the help of one explicit calculation. Namely, according to corollary
5.1
the pair of commuting operators
P_{N,n}^{\prime},Q_{N,n}^{\prime}
corresponding to the pair
\Phi_{N,1}(P_{n}),\Phi_{N,1}(Q_{n})
satisfy an algebraic relation given by some polynomial
f(X,Y)
of BC-type (see definition
5.2
and remark
2.9
). Going to the normal form
\partial^{\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits P_{N,n}^{\prime}},\tilde{Q}_{N,n}^{\prime}
of the pair
P_{N,n}^{\prime},Q_{N,n}^{\prime}
and conjugating the operator
\hat{\Phi}(\tilde{Q}_{N,n}^{\prime})
by the auxiliary operator
\tilde{S}
, we get a Puiseux series
X_{N,n}
which gives a solution of the equation
f(\tilde{D}^{\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits P_{N,n}^{\prime}},X)=0
. This series has a
distinguished coefficient
at
\tilde{D}^{-\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits P_{N,n}^{\prime}}
which is equal to zero according to remark
5.3
(this is an important property which holds for series obtained from commuting operators generating the ring of rank one due to the Newton-Puiseux theorem combined with the Wilson theorem). Because of this property it is possible to find distinguished coefficients of the sequence
X_{N,i}=F_{i}(\tilde{D}^{\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits P_{N,n}^{\prime}},X_{N,n})
– these are coefficients of homogeneous terms corresponding to the beginning of the ”tail” of operators
\tilde{Q}_{N,i}
.

We get a contradiction by showing that, on the one hand side, these distinguished coefficients don’t depend on
N
(lemma
6.3
), and on the other hand side by calculating the distinguished coefficient of the series
X_{N,I}
in Step 7 of section
6.2
(this calculation shows that it depends on
N
).

6.1
Preliminary lemmas

Assume there exists a DC-pair
P,Q\in A_{1}
that provides a counterexample to the Dixmier conjecture and
\varphi
is the corresponding endomorphism with
\varphi(x)=P
,
\varphi(\partial)=Q
. Then, as it was noted in section
3
, there exists a DC-pair
P,Q
of subrectangular type satisfying conditions of theorem
4.1
and conditions of remark
3.2
. Moreover, without loss of generality and by remark
4.1
we can assume

f_{0,1}(P)=(1+\alpha x)^{ln}y^{dn},\quad f_{0,1}(Q)=(1+\alpha x)^{lm}y^{dm},\quad 1<l<d,\quad(m,n)=1,\quad\epsilon(\varphi)\notin{\mbox{\dbl N}}.

Let
f_{1,0}(P)=\tilde{h}x^{ln}
,
\tilde{h}\in{\mbox{\dbl C}}[y]
, then
\tilde{h}=\tilde{g}^{n}
for some
\tilde{g}\in{\mbox{\dbl C}}[y]
of degree
d
and highest coefficient
\alpha^{l}
,
and
f_{1,0}(Q)=\tilde{g}^{m}x^{lm}
(cf. arguments in section
3
).

Remark 6.1
.

Consider operators

\hat{P}=\varphi\circ\Phi_{N,\lambda}(P),\quad\hat{Q}=\varphi\circ\Phi_{N,\lambda}(Q)

from corollary
3.2
. Then, they give another DC-pair, moreover, they also satisfy conditions of theorem
4.1
, as it immediately follows from the proof of lemma

and corollary

. Indeed, we need to check only the condition
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(\hat{P})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\hat{P})
and
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(\hat{Q})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\hat{Q})
(other conditions are trivial). From the proof of lemma
3.2
we know that

HT(\hat{P})=HT(\varphi(\Phi_{N,\lambda}(\varphi(x)))=cHT(\varphi(\partial^{nd+Nln}))=cg^{m(nd+Nln)}

for some
c\in K^{*}
(recall that here
g=(1+\alpha x)^{l}
), so,
HT(\hat{P})(0)\neq 0
and therefore
\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(\hat{P})=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\hat{P})
by remark
2.2
. Analogous arguments work for
\hat{Q}
, since the highest terms of
\hat{Q}
and
\hat{P}
are proportional by
[

, L.2.7]
.

By corollary
3.2
we have

EssGCD(\hat{P},\hat{Q})=EssGCD(P,Q)=:d_{2}>1,\quad\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(\hat{P})=dmn(d+Nl),\quad\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(\hat{Q})=dm^{2}(d+Nl)

(so that for this pair
d
is replaced by
dm(d+Nl)
, and
l
is replaced by
lm(d+Nl)
). So, in particular, we can find a DC-pair with
d\gg 0
.

By theorem
4.1
there is a sequence of polynomials
Q_{i}=F_{i}(X,Y)
,
i=0,\ldots,I
,
such that
SeqGCD_{I}(P,Q)=1
and

. By lemma

we know that

\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{I})=\tilde{M}_{I-1}+1
, where

\tilde{M}_{I-1}=\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{I-1})n_{I-1}-M_{I-1}=\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{I-1})(n_{I-1}-1)+\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{I-2})(n_{I-2}-1)+\ldots+\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{0})(n-1)-dn.

From Items 3.4. 7) of this lemma we also know that
f_{0,1}(Q_{I})=\varepsilon(1+\alpha x)^{\tilde{M}_{I-1}l/d+1}y^{\tilde{M}_{I-1}+1}
for some
\varepsilon\in K^{*}
. Then
f_{1,0}(Q_{I})=H_{1}x^{\tilde{M}_{I-1}l/d+1}
, where

is of degree
\tilde{M}_{I-1}+1
with the highest coefficient
\varepsilon\alpha^{\tilde{M}_{I-1}l/d+1}
.

Lemma 6.1
.

There exists an automorphism
\varphi_{t}\in\mathop{\rm Aut}(A_{1})
(
t\in K
):
\varphi_{t}(\partial)=\partial+c
,
\varphi_{t}(x)=x
, where
c\in K
depends on
t
, such that

\varphi_{t}(\tilde{g})=\alpha^{l}y^{d}+d\alpha^{l}ty^{d-1}+\ldots,\quad\varphi_{t}(H_{1})=\varepsilon\alpha^{\tilde{M}_{I-1}l/d+1}y^{\tilde{M}_{I-1}+1}+(\tilde{M}_{I-1}+1)\varepsilon\alpha^{\tilde{M}_{I-1}l/d+1}ty^{\tilde{M}_{I-1}}+\ldots

where
\ldots
means terms of lower degree.

Proof.

By theorem
4.2
we have two possibilities: either
\tilde{g}
has one root, say
\tilde{g}=(b+\tilde{\alpha}y)^{d}
, or
\tilde{g}
has more than one root.

In the first case
H_{1}=c(b+\tilde{\alpha}y)^{\tilde{M}_{I-1}+1}
,
c\in K
, and our claim is trivial. In the second case
d=ld_{2}
and
H_{1}
satisfies differential equation (
4.11
). Obviously, there is an automorphism
\varphi(\partial)=\partial+c
,
\varphi(x)=x
such that
\varphi(\tilde{g})=\alpha^{l}y^{d}+c_{d-2}y^{d-2}+\ldots
. So, it is sufficient to show that
\varphi(H_{1})
also has zero coefficient at the second place (in this case we can find another automorphism which satisfies our claim).

So, assume
\tilde{g}=\alpha^{l}y^{d}+c_{d-2}y^{d-2}+\ldots
,
H_{1}=h_{0}y^{\tilde{M}_{I-1}+1}+h_{1}y^{\tilde{M}_{I-1}}+\ldots
. From theorem
4.2
we know that
\tilde{M}_{I-1}=((l(M-n)+1)n_{I-1}-1)d_{2}
, and the degree of polynomial

from equation (
4.11
) is
(l(M-n)+1)d_{2}
. Suppose

H=\bar{h}_{0}y^{(l(M-n)+1)d_{2}}+\bar{h}_{1}y^{(l(M-n)+1)d_{2}-1}+\ldots
. Then the equation on the coefficient

from equation (

) is (here we use that

, as

)

\bar{h}_{1}((l(M-n)+1)d_{2}-1)\alpha^{l_{3}}-\bar{h}_{1}((l(M-n)+1)d_{2}\alpha^{l_{3}}=0,

whence
\bar{h}_{1}=0
, and the equation on the coefficient
h_{1}
from equation (
4.11
) is

h_{1}(((l(M-n)+1)n_{I-1}-1)d_{2})\alpha^{l_{3}}-h_{1}((l(M-n)+1)n_{I-1}d_{2}\alpha^{l_{3}}=0,

whence
h_{1}=0
, and we are done.
∎

Consider the automorphism
\psi\in\mathop{\rm Aut}(A_{1})
:

,

.
Then, obviously,

for all

.

Let

(6.1)

Then, clearly,

f_{1,0}(P_{n})=(\alpha y)^{ln}x^{dn},\quad f_{1,0}(Q_{n})=(\alpha y)^{lm}x^{dm},\quad f_{0,1}(P_{n})=\varphi_{t}(\tilde{g})(x)^{n}y^{ln},\quad f_{0,1}(Q_{n})=\varphi_{t}(\tilde{g})(x)^{m}y^{lm}

Lemma 6.2
.

In the notation above (for operators

\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{N,i})=(d_{2}N+l_{2})\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(Q_{i})/d_{2}\quad\mbox{for $i<I$}.

Proof.

Step
1. Obviously, the operators
\Phi_{N,1}(P_{n})
,
\Phi_{N,1}(Q_{n})
are monic,

\sigma(\Phi_{N,1}(P_{n}))=\partial^{dnN+ln},\quad\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(\Phi_{N,1}(P_{n}))=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\Phi_{N,1}(P_{n})),\quad\mathop{\rm ord}\nolimits(\Phi_{N,1}(Q_{n}))=\mathop{\rm\bf ord}(\Phi_{N,1}(Q_{n})).

Let’s denote by

a sequence of polynomials for these operators.
Let’s show that for
i=0,\ldots,I
we can take
\hat{F}_{i}(X,Y)=F_{i}(X,Y)
.

The proof is by induction. For generic
i<I
we need to show that
f_{0,1}(\Phi_{N,1}(F_{i}(P_{n},Q_{n})))^{n_{i}}=\varepsilon_{i}^{n_{i}}f_{0,1}(\Phi_{N,1}(P_{n}))^{m_{i}}

.

From theorem

we know that
F_{i}(P,Q)=Q_{i}
are of subrectangular type,

f_{0,1}(Q_{i})=\varepsilon_{i}(1+\alpha x)^{\tilde{M}}y^{\tilde{N}}

and also

is proportional with

for

. We also know by lemma
4.4
that
